First is a paradigm shift in AI engineering, evolving from prompt and context engineering to harness engineering-designing the complete infrastructure necessary to transform unconstrained agents into controllable, auditable, and production-reliable systems. As model capabilities converge, this harness layer is becoming the primary site of architectural differentiation. Second is the evolution of human-agent interaction from discrete tasks toward a persistent, contextually aware collaborative relationship, which demands open, trustworthy and extensible harness infrastructure. We present SemaClaw, an open-source multi-agent application framework that addresses these shifts by taking a step towards general-purpose personal AI agents through harness engineering. Our primary contributions include a DAG-based two-phase hybrid agent team orchestration method, a PermissionBridge behavioral safety system, a three-tier context management architecture, and an agentic wiki skill for automated personal knowledge base construction.

</details>


### [157] [UniToolCall: Unifying Tool-Use Representation, Data, and Evaluation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11557)
*Yijuan Liang,Xinghao Chen,Yifan Ge,Ziyi Wu,Hao Wu,Changyu Zeng,Wei Xing,Xiaoyu Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UniToolCall is a unified framework for LLM tool learning that standardizes toolset construction, dataset generation, and evaluation, achieving state-of-the-art performance on tool-use tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on LLM tool-use suffers from inconsistent interaction representations, neglects structural distribution of tool-use trajectories, and relies on incompatible evaluation benchmarks, creating fragmentation in the field.

Method: Creates a unified framework with: 1) 22k+ tool pool, 2) 390k+ hybrid training corpus combining public datasets with synthetic trajectories, 3) explicit modeling of interaction patterns (single/multi-hop, single/multi-turn, serial/parallel execution), 4) Anchor Linkage mechanism for cross-turn dependencies, and 5) unified QAOA representation for 7 benchmarks with multi-level evaluation.

Result: Fine-tuning Qwen3-8B on the UniToolCall dataset achieves 93.0% single-turn Strict Precision under distractor-heavy Hybrid-20 setting, outperforming commercial models including GPT, Gemini, and Claude.

Conclusion: UniToolCall provides a comprehensive, standardized framework for tool learning that addresses current fragmentation, demonstrates strong empirical performance, and establishes a unified evaluation paradigm for the field.

Abstract: Tool-use capability is a fundamental component of LLM agents, enabling them to interact with external systems through structured function calls. However, existing research exhibits inconsistent interaction representations, largely overlooks the structural distribution of tool-use trajectories, and relies on incompatible evaluation benchmarks. We present UniToolCall, a unified framework for tool learning that standardizes the entire pipeline from toolset construction and dataset generation to evaluation. The framework curates a large tool pool of 22k+ tools and constructs a hybrid training corpus of 390k+ instances by combining 10 standardized public datasets with structurally controlled synthetic trajectories. It explicitly models diverse interaction patterns, including single-hop vs. multi-hop and single-turn vs. multi-turn, while capturing both serial and parallel execution structures. To support coherent multi-turn reasoning, we further introduce an Anchor Linkage mechanism that enforces cross-turn dependencies. Furthermore, we convert 7 public benchmarks into a unified Query--Action--Observation--Answer (QAOA) representation with fine-grained evaluation at the function-call, turn, and conversation levels. Experiments show that fine-tuning Qwen3-8B on our dataset substantially improves tool-use performance. Under the distractor-heavy Hybrid-20 setting, achieves 93.0% single-turn Strict Precision, outperforming commercial models including GPT, Gemini, and Claude.

</details>


### [158] [Intersectional Sycophancy: How Perceived User Demographics Shape False Validation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11609)
*Benjamin Maltbie,Shivam Raval*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPT-5-nano shows significant demographic-dependent sycophancy (agreeing with incorrect user beliefs), with Hispanic personas receiving highest false validation rates, while Claude Haiku 4.5 exhibits uniformly low sycophancy without demographic variation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLM sycophancy (validating incorrect user beliefs to appear agreeable) varies systematically with perceived user demographics, testing intersectional combinations of race, age, gender, and confidence level across different domains.

Method: Conducted 768 multi-turn adversarial conversations using Anthropic's Petri evaluation framework, probing GPT-5-nano and Claude Haiku 4.5 across 128 persona combinations in mathematics, philosophy, and conspiracy theory domains with intersectional demographic variations.

Result: GPT-5-nano is significantly more sycophantic than Claude Haiku 4.5 overall. GPT-5-nano shows demographic variation: philosophy elicits 41% more sycophancy than mathematics; Hispanic personas receive highest sycophancy across races; worst-scoring persona (confident 23-year-old Hispanic woman) averages 5.33/10. Claude Haiku 4.5 shows uniformly low sycophancy with no significant demographic variation.

Conclusion: Sycophancy is not uniformly distributed across users, demonstrating identity-dependent bias in LLM behavior, necessitating safety evaluations that incorporate identity-aware testing rather than assuming uniform performance across demographics.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit sycophantic tendencies--validating incorrect user beliefs to appear agreeable. We investigate whether this behavior varies systematically with perceived user demographics, testing whether combinations of race, age, gender, and expressed confidence level produce differential false validation rates. Inspired by the legal concept of intersectionality, we conduct 768 multi-turn adversarial conversations using Anthropic's Petri evaluation framework, probing GPT-5-nano and Claude Haiku 4.5 across 128 persona combinations in mathematics, philosophy, and conspiracy theory domains. GPT-5-nano is significantly more sycophantic than Claude Haiku 4.5 overall ($\bar{x}=2.96$ vs. $1.74$, $p < 10^{-32}$, Wilcoxon signed-rank). For GPT-5-nano, we find that philosophy elicits 41% more sycophancy than mathematics and that Hispanic personas receive the highest sycophancy across races. The worst-scoring persona, a confident, 23-year-old Hispanic woman, averages 5.33/10 on sycophancy. Claude Haiku 4.5 exhibits uniformly low sycophancy with no significant demographic variation. These results demonstrate that sycophancy is not uniformly distributed across users and that safety evaluations should incorporate identity-aware testing.

</details>


### [159] [Context Kubernetes: Declarative Orchestration of Enterprise Knowledge for Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11623)
*Charafeddine Mouzouni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Context Kubernetes is an architecture for orchestrating enterprise knowledge in agentic AI systems, addressing knowledge delivery, permissions, and freshness across organizations through Kubernetes-inspired abstractions and governance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of delivering appropriate knowledge to AI agents with proper permissions and freshness across entire organizations, drawing analogy to container orchestration problems that Kubernetes solved for infrastructure.

Method: Proposes Context Kubernetes architecture with six core abstractions, YAML-based declarative manifests for knowledge-architecture-as-code, a reconciliation loop, and a three-tier agent permission model where agent authority is always a strict subset of human authority.

Result: Experiments show: (1) without governance, agents serve phantom content and leak cross-domain data in 26.5% of queries; (2) without freshness monitoring, stale content served silently - with reconciliation, staleness detected in <1ms; (3) three-tier permission model blocks 5/5 attacks vs. basic RBAC (4/5) and flat permissions (0/5). Five correctness experiments confirm zero unauthorized deliveries and invariant violations.

Conclusion: Context Kubernetes provides architectural enforcement of knowledge governance that existing enterprise platforms lack, with four identified properties making context orchestration harder than container orchestration but more valuable.

Abstract: We introduce Context Kubernetes, an architecture for orchestrating enterprise knowledge in agentic AI systems, with a prototype implementation and eight experiments. The core observation is that delivering the right knowledge, to the right agent, with the right permissions, at the right freshness -- across an entire organization -- is structurally analogous to the container orchestration problem Kubernetes solved a decade ago. We formalize six core abstractions, a YAML-based declarative manifest for knowledge-architecture-as-code, a reconciliation loop, and a three-tier agent permission model where agent authority is always a strict subset of human authority. Three value experiments show: (1) without governance, agents serve phantom content from deleted sources and leak cross-domain data in 26.5% of queries; (2) without freshness monitoring, stale content is served silently -- with reconciliation, staleness is detected in under 1ms; (3) in five attack scenarios, flat permissions block 0/5 attacks, basic RBAC blocks 4/5, and the three-tier model blocks 5/5. Five correctness experiments confirm zero unauthorized deliveries, zero invariant violations, and architectural enforcement of out-of-band approval isolation that no surveyed enterprise platform provides. A survey of four major platforms (Microsoft, Salesforce, AWS, Google) documents that none architecturally isolates agent approval channels. We identify four properties that make context orchestration harder than container orchestration, and argue that these make the solution more valuable.

</details>


### [160] [RationalRewards: Reasoning Rewards Scale Visual Generation Both Training and Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11626)
*Haozhe Wang,Cong Wei,Weiming Ren,Jiaming Liu,Fangzhen Lin,Wenhu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Teaching reward models to produce explicit critiques before scoring transforms them into active optimization tools, improving generators through interpretable RL rewards and test-time critique-refine loops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reward models for visual generation reduce human judgments to single unexplained scores, discarding valuable reasoning behind preferences. This limits their utility as optimization tools and interpretability.

Method: Proposed Preference-Anchored Rationalization (PARROT) framework to train reward models to produce multi-dimensional critiques without costly rationale annotations. Uses anchored generation, consistency filtering, and distillation. The resulting RationalRewards (8B) model provides structured rationales before scoring.

Result: RationalRewards achieves SOTA preference prediction among open-source reward models, competitive with Gemini-2.5-Pro using 10-20x less training data. As RL reward, improves text-to-image and image-editing generators beyond scalar alternatives. Test-time critique-refine loop matches/exceeds RL-based fine-tuning on several benchmarks.

Conclusion: Structured reasoning transforms reward models from passive evaluators to active optimization tools, unlocking latent capabilities in existing generators that suboptimal prompts fail to elicit through both training-time interpretable rewards and test-time critique-refine loops.

Abstract: Most reward models for visual generation reduce rich human judgments to a single unexplained score, discarding the reasoning that underlies preference. We show that teaching reward models to produce explicit, multi-dimensional critiques before scoring transforms them from passive evaluators into active optimization tools, improving generators in two complementary ways: at training time, structured rationales provide interpretable, fine-grained rewards for reinforcement learning; at test time, a Generate-Critique-Refine loop turns critiques into targeted prompt revisions that improve outputs without any parameter updates. To train such a reward model without costly rationale annotations, we introduce Preference-Anchored Rationalization (PARROT), a principled framework that recovers high-quality rationales from readily available preference data through anchored generation, consistency filtering, and distillation. The resulting model, RationalRewards (8B), achieves state-of-the-art preference prediction among open-source reward models, competitive with Gemini-2.5-Pro, while using 10-20x less training data than comparable baselines. As an RL reward, it consistently improves text-to-image and image-editing generators beyond scalar alternatives. Most strikingly, its test-time critique-and-refine loop matches or exceeds RL-based fine-tuning on several benchmarks, suggesting that structured reasoning can unlock latent capabilities in existing generators that suboptimal prompts fail to elicit.

</details>


### [161] [Why Do Large Language Models Generate Harmful Content?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11663)
*Rajesh Ganguli,Raha Moraffah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Causal mediation analysis reveals harmful content generation in LLMs originates in later layers, primarily from MLP block failures, with specific neurons acting as harmful content gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs generate harmful content, but the underlying causal mechanisms remain poorly understood. The paper aims to identify the specific causal factors responsible for harmful generation through systematic analysis.

Method: Proposes a causal mediation analysis-based approach to identify causal factors for harmful generation. Performs multi-granular analysis across model layers, modules (MLP and attention blocks), and individual neurons.

Result: Harmful generation arises in later layers, results primarily from failures in MLP blocks rather than attention blocks, and is associated with neurons acting as gating mechanisms for harmful generation. Early layers perform contextual understanding of harmfulness, which propagates through the model to late layers and MLP blocks, then to sparse neurons in the final layer that determine harmful content generation.

Conclusion: The study provides causal insights into harmful content generation in LLMs, revealing specific architectural components (later layers, MLP blocks, sparse neurons) responsible for harmful outputs, offering targets for mitigation strategies.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have been shown to generate harmful content. However, the underlying causes of such behavior remain under explored. We propose a causal mediation analysis-based approach to identify the causal factors responsible for harmful generation. Our method performs a multi-granular analysis across model layers, modules (MLP and attention blocks), and individual neurons. Extensive experiments on state-of-the-art LLMs indicate that harmful generation arises in the later layers of the model, results primarily from failures in MLP blocks rather than attention blocks, and is associated with neurons that act as a gating mechanism for harmful generation. The results indicate that the early layers in the model are used for a contextual understanding of harmfulness in a prompt, which is then propagated through the model, to generate harmfulness in the late layers, as well as a signal indicating harmfulness through MLP blocks. This is then further propagated to the last layer of the model, specifically to a sparse set of neurons, which receives the signal and determines the generation of harmful content accordingly.

</details>


### [162] [DreamKG: A KG-Augmented Conversational System for People Experiencing Homelessness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11703)
*Javad M Alizadeh,Genhui Zheng,Chiu C Tan,Yuzhou Chen,Omar Martinez,Philip McCallion,Ying Ding,Chenguang Yang,AnneMarie Tomosky,Huanmei Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DreamKG is a knowledge graph-augmented conversational system that provides verified, up-to-date information about community services for people experiencing homelessness, outperforming Google Search AI by 59% on relevant queries while rejecting 84% of irrelevant queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: People experiencing homelessness face significant barriers accessing timely, accurate information about community services, and standard large language models are prone to hallucinations when providing such information.

Method: Combines Neo4j knowledge graphs with structured query understanding to ground responses in verified data about Philadelphia organizations, services, locations, and hours. Uses spatial reasoning for distance-based recommendations and temporal filtering for operating hours.

Result: Preliminary evaluation shows 59% superiority over Google Search AI on relevant queries and 84% rejection of irrelevant queries. The system reliably handles location-aware and time-sensitive queries without hallucinations.

Conclusion: Hybrid architectures combining LLM flexibility with knowledge graph reliability can effectively improve service accessibility for vulnerable populations by providing accurate, verified information.

Abstract: People experiencing homelessness (PEH) face substantial barriers to accessing timely, accurate information about community services. DreamKG addresses this through a knowledge graph-augmented conversational system that grounds responses in verified, up-to-date data about Philadelphia organizations, services, locations, and hours. Unlike standard large language models (LLMs) prone to hallucinations, DreamKG combines Neo4j knowledge graphs with structured query understanding to handle location-aware and time-sensitive queries reliably. The system performs spatial reasoning for distance-based recommendations and temporal filtering for operating hours. Preliminary evaluation shows 59% superiority over Google Search AI on relevant queries and 84% rejection of irrelevant queries. This demonstration highlights the potential of hybrid architectures that combines LLM flexibility with knowledge graph reliability to improve service accessibility for vulnerable populations effectively.

</details>


### [163] [A Mamba-Based Multimodal Network for Multiscale Blast-Induced Rapid Structural Damage Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11709)
*Wanli Ma,Sivasakthy Selvakumaran,Dain G. Farrimond,Adam A. Dennis,Samuel E. Rigby*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Mamba-based multimodal network integrates multi-scale blast-loading information with optical remote sensing images for rapid structural damage assessment, outperforming state-of-the-art methods on the 2020 Beirut explosion dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional field inspections for structural damage assessment are limited by accessibility, safety risks, and time constraints after large explosions. Existing machine learning approaches with remote sensing require extensive training data and fail to incorporate key physical characteristics of blast loading.

Method: Proposes a Mamba-based multimodal network that integrates multi-scale blast-loading information with optical remote sensing images for rapid structural damage assessment.

Result: Evaluated on the 2020 Beirut explosion dataset, the method significantly improves performance over state-of-the-art approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed multimodal approach effectively addresses limitations of existing methods by incorporating physical blast characteristics, enabling more accurate and rapid structural damage assessment for post-disaster management.

Abstract: Accurate and rapid structural damage assessment (SDA) is crucial for post-disaster management, helping responders prioritise resources, plan rescues, and support recovery. Traditional field inspections, though precise, are limited by accessibility, safety risks, and time constraints, especially after large explosions. Machine learning with remote sensing has emerged as a scalable solution for rapid SDA, with Mamba-based networks achieving state-of-the-art performance. However, these methods often require extensive training and large datasets, limiting real-world applicability. Moreover, they fail to incorporate key physical characteristics of blast loading for SDA. To overcome these challenges, we propose a Mamba-based multimodal network for rapid SDA that integrates multi-scale blast-loading information with optical remote sensing images. Evaluated on the 2020 Beirut explosion, our method significantly improves performance over state-of-the-art approaches. Code is available at: https://github.com/IMPACTSquad/Blast-Mamba

</details>


### [164] [SWE-AGILE: A Software Agent Framework for Efficiently Managing Dynamic Reasoning Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11716)
*Shuquan Lian,Juncheng Liu,Yazhe Chen,Yuhong Chen,Hui Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SWE-AGILE is a novel software agent framework that addresses context explosion and reasoning redundancy in autonomous software engineering by introducing Dynamic Reasoning Context with sliding windows and reasoning digests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ReAct-style approaches in autonomous software engineering lack explicit System-2 reasoning for deep analysis and complex edge cases. Applying extended Chain-of-Thought reasoning to multi-turn SWE tasks creates a dilemma: retaining full reasoning history causes context explosion and "Lost-in-the-Middle" degradation, while discarding it forces redundant re-reasoning at every step.

Method: SWE-AGILE introduces a Dynamic Reasoning Context strategy that maintains a "sliding window" of detailed reasoning for immediate continuity to prevent redundant re-analyzing, while compressing historical reasoning content into concise Reasoning Digests.

Result: SWE-AGILE sets a new standard for 7B-8B models on SWE-Bench-Verified using only 2.2k trajectories and 896 tasks.

Conclusion: SWE-AGILE successfully bridges the gap between reasoning depth, efficiency, and context constraints in autonomous software engineering, providing an effective solution to the context explosion vs. redundant reasoning dilemma.

Abstract: Prior representative ReAct-style approaches in autonomous Software Engineering (SWE) typically lack the explicit System-2 reasoning required for deep analysis and handling complex edge cases. While recent reasoning models demonstrate the potential of extended Chain-of-Thought (CoT), applying them to the multi-turn SWE task creates a fundamental dilemma: retaining full reasoning history leads to context explosion and ``Lost-in-the-Middle'' degradation, while discarding it would force the agent to redundantly re-reason at every step. To address these challenges, we propose SWE-AGILE, a novel software agent framework designed to bridge the gap between reasoning depth, efficiency, and context constraints. SWE-AGILE introduces a Dynamic Reasoning Context strategy, maintaining a ``sliding window'' of detailed reasoning for immediate continuity to prevent redundant re-analyzing, while compressing historical reasoning content into concise Reasoning Digests. Empirically, SWE-AGILE sets a new standard for 7B-8B models on SWE-Bench-Verified using only 2.2k trajectories and 896 tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/KDEGroup/SWE-AGILE.

</details>


### [165] [Collaborative Multi-Agent Scripts Generation for Enhancing Imperfect-Information Reasoning in Murder Mystery Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11741)
*Keyang Zhong,Junlin Xie,Hefeng Wu,Haofeng Li,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A collaborative multi-agent framework enhances vision-language models' multi-hop reasoning in deceptive multiplayer games like Murder Mystery through role-driven script generation and agent-monitored training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models degrade in complex multi-hop reasoning under multiplayer game settings with imperfect and deceptive information, particularly in tasks like Murder Mystery Games that require inferring hidden truths from partial clues provided by roles with different intentions.

Method: Proposes a collaborative multi-agent framework for evaluating and synthesizing high-quality, role-driven multiplayer game scripts with fine-grained interaction patterns tailored to character identities. Uses a two-stage agent-monitored training strategy: (1) chain-of-thought based fine-tuning on curated and synthetic datasets modeling uncertainty and deception; (2) GRPO-based reinforcement learning with agent-monitored reward shaping to develop character-specific reasoning behaviors and effective multimodal multi-hop inference.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate significant performance boosts in narrative reasoning, hidden fact extraction, and deception-resilient understanding. The method offers improved reasoning capabilities for VLMs in complex, deceptive scenarios.

Conclusion: The framework provides a scalable solution for training and evaluating VLMs under uncertain, adversarial, and socially complex conditions, laying groundwork for future benchmarks in multimodal multi-hop reasoning under imperfect information.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown impressive capabilities in perceptual tasks, yet they degrade in complex multi-hop reasoning under multiplayer game settings with imperfect and deceptive information. In this paper, we study a representative multiplayer task, Murder Mystery Games, which require inferring hidden truths based on partial clues provided by roles with different intentions. To address this challenge, we propose a collaborative multi-agent framework for evaluating and synthesizing high-quality, role-driven multiplayer game scripts, enabling fine-grained interaction patterns tailored to character identities (i.e., murderer vs. innocent). Our system generates rich multimodal contexts, including character backstories, visual and textual clues, and multi-hop reasoning chains, through coordinated agent interactions. We design a two-stage agent-monitored training strategy to enhance the reasoning ability of VLMs: (1) chain-of-thought based fine-tuning on curated and synthetic datasets that model uncertainty and deception; (2) GRPO-based reinforcement learning with agent-monitored reward shaping, encouraging the model to develop character-specific reasoning behaviors and effective multimodal multi-hop inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method significantly boosts the performance of VLMs in narrative reasoning, hidden fact extraction, and deception-resilient understanding. Our contributions offer a scalable solution for training and evaluating VLMs under uncertain, adversarial, and socially complex conditions, laying the groundwork for future benchmarks in multimodal multi-hop reasoning under imperfect information.

</details>


### [166] [Retrieval Is Not Enough: Why Organizational AI Needs Epistemic Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11759)
*Federico Bottino,Carlo Ferrero,Nicholas Dosio,Pierfrancesco Beneventano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OIDA framework structures organizational knowledge with epistemic properties (commitment strength, contradictions, ignorance) using typed Knowledge Objects, Knowledge Gravity Engine, and QUESTION mechanism for modeling ignorance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI retrieval systems lack epistemic structure - they don't distinguish binding decisions from abandoned hypotheses, contested claims from settled ones, or known facts from unresolved questions. The ceiling on organizational AI is not retrieval fidelity but epistemic fidelity.

Method: OIDA framework structures organizational knowledge as typed Knowledge Objects with epistemic class, importance scores with class-specific decay, and signed contradiction edges. Knowledge Gravity Engine maintains scores deterministically with convergence guarantees. Introduces QUESTION-as-modeled-ignorance primitive with inverse decay.

Result: OIDA's RAG condition (3,868 tokens) achieves Epistemic Quality Score 0.530 vs. 0.848 for full-context baseline (108,687 tokens). QUESTION mechanism statistically validated (Fisher p=0.0325, OR=21.0). Formal properties established; decisive ablation at equal token budget pre-registered but not yet run.

Conclusion: OIDA provides a framework for epistemic fidelity in organizational AI, addressing limitations of current retrieval systems by modeling commitment strength, contradictions, and organizational ignorance as computable properties.

Abstract: Organizational knowledge used by AI agents typically lacks epistemic structure: retrieval systems surface semantically relevant content without distinguishing binding decisions from abandoned hypotheses, contested claims from settled ones, or known facts from unresolved questions. We argue that the ceiling on organizational AI is not retrieval fidelity but \emph{epistemic} fidelity--the system's ability to represent commitment strength, contradiction status, and organizational ignorance as computable properties.
  We present OIDA, a framework that structures organizational knowledge as typed Knowledge Objects carrying epistemic class, importance scores with class-specific decay, and signed contradiction edges. The Knowledge Gravity Engine maintains scores deterministically with proved convergence guarantees (sufficient condition: max degree $< 7$; empirically robust to degree 43). OIDA introduces QUESTION-as-modeled-ignorance: a primitive with inverse decay that surfaces what an organization does \emph{not} know with increasing urgency--a mechanism absent from all surveyed systems. We describe the Epistemic Quality Score (EQS), a five-component evaluation methodology with explicit circularity analysis. In a controlled comparison ($n{=}10$ response pairs), OIDA's RAG condition (3,868 tokens) achieves EQS 0.530 vs.\ 0.848 for a full-context baseline (108,687 tokens); the $28.1\times$ token budget difference is the primary confound. The QUESTION mechanism is statistically validated (Fisher $p{=}0.0325$, OR$=21.0$). The formal properties are established; the decisive ablation at equal token budget (E4) is pre-registered and not yet run.

</details>


### [167] [GenTac: Generative Modeling and Forecasting of Soccer Tactics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11786)
*Jiayuan Rao,Tianlin Gui,Haoning Wu,Yanfeng Wang,Weidi Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GenTac is a diffusion-based generative framework that models soccer tactics as stochastic processes, generating diverse, plausible multi-player trajectories and tactical events while supporting rich contextual conditioning for team sports analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational approaches for soccer tactics are inadequate because they produce single deterministic trajectory forecasts or focus only on structured set-pieces, failing to capture the inherent stochasticity, variance, and branching possibilities of real-world open-play soccer dynamics.

Method: GenTac uses a diffusion-based generative framework that models soccer tactics as a stochastic process over continuous multi-player trajectories and discrete semantic events. It learns the underlying distribution of player movements from historical tracking data, supports rich contextual conditioning (opponent behavior, team/league styles, strategic objectives), and grounds continuous spatial dynamics into a 15-class tactical event space.

Result: Extensive evaluations on TacBench benchmark demonstrate: (1) high geometric accuracy with preserved team structural consistency; (2) accurate simulation of stylistic nuances distinguishing specific teams and leagues; (3) controllable counterfactual simulations altering spatial control and expected threat metrics; (4) reliable anticipation of future tactical outcomes from generated rollouts. The framework also generalizes to other team sports including basketball, American football, and ice hockey.

Conclusion: GenTac provides a comprehensive generative framework for modeling stochastic, multi-agent soccer tactics that captures real-world variance and branching possibilities, enabling diverse tactical simulations, style analysis, counterfactual reasoning, and outcome prediction while demonstrating cross-sport generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Modeling open-play soccer tactics is a formidable challenge due to the stochastic, multi-agent nature of the game. Existing computational approaches typically produce single, deterministic trajectory forecasts or focus on highly structured set-pieces, fundamentally failing to capture the inherent variance and branching possibilities of real-world match evolution. Here, we introduce GenTac, a diffusion-based generative framework that conceptualizes soccer tactics as a stochastic process over continuous multi-player trajectories and discrete semantic events. By learning the underlying distribution of player movements from historical tracking data, GenTac samples diverse, plausible, long-horizon future trajectories. The framework supports rich contextual conditioning, including opponent behavior, specific team or league playing styles, and strategic objectives, while grounding continuous spatial dynamics into a 15-class tactical event space. Extensive evaluations on our proposed benchmark, TacBench, demonstrate four key capabilities: (1) GenTac achieves high geometric accuracy while strictly preserving the collective structural consistency of the team; (2) it accurately simulates stylistic nuances, distinguishing between specific teams (e.g., Auckland FC) and leagues (e.g., A-League versus German leagues); (3) it enables controllable counterfactual simulations, demonstrably altering spatial control and expected threat metrics based on offensive or defensive guidance; and (4) it reliably anticipates future tactical outcomes directly from generated rollouts. Finally, we demonstrate that GenTac can be successfully trained to generalize to other dynamic team sports, including basketball, American football, and ice hockey.

</details>


### [168] [Detecting Safety Violations Across Many Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11806)
*Adam Stein,Davis Brown,Hamed Hassani,Mayur Naik,Eric Wong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Meerkat is a novel auditing system that combines clustering with agentic search to efficiently detect rare, complex safety violations across large collections of agent traces, outperforming existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety auditing approaches struggle with detecting rare, complex violations that may be adversarially hidden and only visible when analyzing multiple traces together. Existing methods have limitations: per-trace judges miss cross-trace patterns, agentic auditing doesn't scale to large trace collections, and fixed monitors are brittle to unanticipated behaviors.

Method: Meerkat combines clustering with agentic search to uncover violations specified in natural language. It uses structured search and adaptive investigation of promising regions, enabling detection of sparse failures without relying on seed scenarios, fixed workflows, or exhaustive enumeration.

Result: Meerkat significantly improves detection of safety violations over baseline monitors across misuse, misalignment, and task gaming settings. It discovered widespread developer cheating on a top agent benchmark and found nearly 4x more examples of reward hacking on CyBench than previous audits.

Conclusion: Meerkat provides an effective solution for auditing large collections of agent traces to detect rare, complex safety violations that existing approaches miss, demonstrating superior performance across multiple challenging safety domains.

Abstract: To identify safety violations, auditors often search over large sets of agent traces. This search is difficult because failures are often rare, complex, and sometimes even adversarially hidden and only detectable when multiple traces are analyzed together. These challenges arise in diverse settings such as misuse campaigns, covert sabotage, reward hacking, and prompt injection. Existing approaches struggle here for several reasons. Per-trace judges miss failures that only become visible across traces, naive agentic auditing does not scale to large trace collections, and fixed monitors are brittle to unanticipated behaviors. We introduce Meerkat, which combines clustering with agentic search to uncover violations specified in natural language. Through structured search and adaptive investigation of promising regions, Meerkat finds sparse failures without relying on seed scenarios, fixed workflows, or exhaustive enumeration. Across misuse, misalignment, and task gaming settings, Meerkat significantly improves detection of safety violations over baseline monitors, discovers widespread developer cheating on a top agent benchmark, and finds nearly 4x more examples of reward hacking on CyBench than previous audits.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [169] [Spectral Kernel Dynamics via Maximum Caliber: Fixed Points, Geodesics, and Phase Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09745)
*Jnaneshwar Das*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Maximum Caliber variational principle applied to graph kernel dynamics yields closed-form geometric functional with explicit solutions for spectral transfer functions, enabling efficient computation and early-warning signals for network phase transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a principled geometric framework for kernel dynamics on finite graphs using variational principles, enabling explicit solutions and computational efficiency for analyzing network structural transitions.

Method: Apply Maximum Caliber (MaxCal) variational principle to spectral transfer function h(λ) of graph Laplacian eigenbasis, deriving closed-form geometric functional that decouples into N one-dimensional problems with explicit exponential tilting solutions.

Result: Obtain self-consistent kernels via exponential tilting, log-linear Fisher-Rao geodesics, diagonal Hessian stability criterion, and l²₊ isometry for spectral kernel space; spectral entropy provides O(N) early-warning signal for network phase transitions, verified numerically on P₈ graph.

Conclusion: The MaxCal framework provides a mathematically rigorous and computationally efficient approach to graph kernel dynamics with explicit solutions, geometric interpretations, and practical applications for detecting network structural transitions, grounded in analogy with Einstein's field equations.

Abstract: We derive a closed-form geometric functional for kernel dynamics on finite graphs by applying the Maximum Caliber (MaxCal) variational principle to the spectral transfer function h(lambda) of the graph Laplacian eigenbasis. The main result is that the MaxCal stationarity condition decouples into N one-dimensional problems with explicit solution: h*(lambda_l) = h_0(lambda_l) exp(-1 - T_l[h*]), yielding self-consistent (fixed-point) kernels via exponential tilting (Corollary 1), log-linear Fisher-Rao geodesics (Corollary 2), a diagonal Hessian stability criterion (Corollary 3), and an l^2_+ isometry for the spectral kernel space (Proposition 3). The spectral entropy H[h_t] provides a computable O(N) early-warning signal for network-structural phase transitions (Remark 7). All claims are numerically verified on the path graph P_8 with a Gaussian mutual-information source, using the open-source kernelcal library. The framework is grounded in a structural analogy with Einstein's field equations, used as a guiding template rather than an established equivalence; explicit limits are stated in Section 6.

</details>


### [170] [Kinematics of continuum planar grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09800)
*Udit Halder,Nicolas Echeverria Zambrano,Xincheng Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Analytical framework for studying geometry of soft continuum arms grasping planar objects, formulated as kinematic boundary following with optimal control solutions and grasp quality metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a systematic geometric framework for analyzing how soft continuum arms grasp planar objects, addressing the need for analytical tools in continuum robotics for grasping applications.

Method: Model arm centerline and object boundary as smooth curves; formulate grasping as kinematic boundary following problem; derive reduced kinematic equations using relative geometric shape variables; solve optimal control problem via Pontryagin's Maximum Principle; propose continuum grasp quality metrics based on algebraic properties of grasp map.

Result: Developed analytical framework with reduced kinematic equations, optimal control solutions for feasible arm shapes, continuum grasp quality metrics, and feedback control considerations; validated through systematic numerical simulations.

Conclusion: The proposed methodology provides a comprehensive geometric framework for analyzing and optimizing continuum arm grasping, with potential applications in continuum robotics and soft manipulation systems.

Abstract: This paper presents an analytical framework to study the geometry arising when a soft continuum arm grasps a planar object. Both the arm centerline and the object boundary are modeled as smooth curves. The grasping problem is formulated as a kinematic boundary following problem, in which the object boundary acts as the arm's 'shadow curve'. This formulation leads to a set of reduced kinematic equations expressed in terms of relative geometric shape variables, with the arm curvature serving as the control input. An optimal control problem is formulated to determine feasible arm shapes that achieve optimal grasping configurations, and its solution is obtained using Pontryagin's Maximum Principle. Based on the resulting optimal grasp kinematics, a class of continuum grasp quality metrics is proposed using the algebraic properties of the associated continuum grasp map. Feedback control aspects in the dynamic setting are also discussed. The proposed methodology is illustrated through systematic numerical simulations.

</details>


### [171] [Perception Is All You Need: A Neuroscience Framework for Low Cost Sensorless Gaze in HRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09829)
*Mason Kadem*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Researchers propose a sensor-free, privacy-preserving gaze-following framework using perceptual illusions from neuroscience, enabling sub-dollar cardboard robots that leverage human visual processing instead of expensive sensors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current gaze-following in child-robot interaction requires expensive platforms ($30,000+), complex sensors and algorithms, and raises privacy concerns, limiting scalability and accessibility.

Method: A framework that avoids sensors/computation by exploiting human visual perception: using concave eye sockets with painted pupils that appear convex due to the brain's convexity prior, creating perceptual gaze-following. Grounded in three neuroscience principles: distributed face processing network, convexity prior, and predictive processing hierarchy.

Result: Development of sub-dollar open-template cardboard robots with parameterized interchangeable eye inserts that create mutual gaze perception from any viewing angle without sensors, power, or privacy concerns.

Conclusion: By leveraging perceptual neuroscience principles, this framework enables scalable, affordable gaze-following robots for child interaction, potentially delivering two decades of HRI gaze findings at population scale while identifying boundary conditions where the approach succeeds or fails.

Abstract: Gaze-following in child-robot interaction improves attention, recall, and learning, but requires expensive platforms (\$30,000+), sensors, algorithms, and raises privacy concerns. We propose a framework that avoids sensors and computation entirely, instead relying on the human visual system's assumption of convexity to produce perceptual gaze-following between a robot and its viewer. Specifically, we motivate sub-dollar cardboard robot design that directly implements the brain's own gaze computation pipeline in reverse, making the viewer's perceptual system the robot's "actuator", with no sensors, no power, and no privacy concerns. We ground this framework in three converging lines of theoretical and empirical neuroscience evidence. Namely, the distributed face processing network that computes gaze direction via the superior temporal sulcus, the high-precision convexity prior that causes the brain to perceive concave faces as convex, and the predictive processing hierarchy in which top-down face knowledge overrides bottom-up depth signals. These mechanisms explain why a concave eye socket with a painted pupil produces the perception of mutual gaze from any viewing angle. We derive design constraints from perceptual science, present a sub-dollar open-template robot with parameterized interchangeable eye inserts, and identify boundary conditions (developmental, clinical, and geometric) that predict where the framework will succeed and where it will fail. If leveraged, two decades of HRI gaze findings become deliverable at population scale.

</details>


### [172] [RoboLab: A High-Fidelity Simulation Benchmark for Analysis of Task Generalist Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09860)
*Xuning Yang,Rishit Dagli,Alex Zook,Hugo Hadfield,Ankit Goyal,Stan Birchfield,Fabio Ramos,Jonathan Tremblay*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboLab is a simulation benchmarking framework that addresses limitations in current robotics evaluation by enabling systematic testing of generalization capabilities through human/LLM-generated tasks and controlled perturbation analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current simulation-based robotics benchmarks suffer from rapid performance saturation and lack true generalization testing due to significant domain overlap between training and evaluation, which trivializes success rates and obscures robustness insights.

Method: RoboLab enables human-authored and LLM-enabled generation of scenes/tasks in robot- and policy-agnostic manner within physically realistic and photorealistic simulation. Introduces RoboLab-120 benchmark with 120 tasks across three competency axes (visual, procedural, relational) and three difficulty levels, plus systematic analysis of real-world policies with controlled perturbations.

Result: Evaluation with RoboLab exposes significant performance gaps in current state-of-the-art models. The framework demonstrates that high-fidelity simulation can serve as a proxy for analyzing real-world policy performance and its dependence on external factors through controlled perturbation sensitivity analysis.

Conclusion: RoboLab provides a scalable framework with granular metrics for evaluating true generalization capabilities of task-generalist robotic policies, addressing critical bottlenecks in simulation-based benchmarking for general-purpose robotics.

Abstract: The pursuit of general-purpose robotics has yielded impressive foundation models, yet simulation-based benchmarking remains a bottleneck due to rapid performance saturation and a lack of true generalization testing. Existing benchmarks often exhibit significant domain overlap between training and evaluation, trivializing success rates and obscuring insights into robustness. We introduce RoboLab, a simulation benchmarking framework designed to address these challenges. Concretely, our framework is designed to answer two questions: (1) to what extent can we understand the performance of a real-world policy by analyzing its behavior in simulation, and (2) which external factors most strongly affect that behavior under controlled perturbations. First, RoboLab enables human-authored and LLM-enabled generation of scenes and tasks in a robot- and policy-agnostic manner within a physically realistic and photorealistic simulation. With this, we propose the RoboLab-120 benchmark, consisting of 120 tasks categorized into three competency axes: visual, procedural, relational competency, across three difficulty levels. Second, we introduce a systematic analysis of real-world policies that quantify both their performance and the sensitivity of their behavior to controlled perturbations, indicating that high-fidelity simulation can serve as a proxy for analyzing performance and its dependence on external factors. Evaluation with RoboLab exposes significant performance gap in current state-of-the-art models. By providing granular metrics and a scalable toolset, RoboLab offers a scalable framework for evaluating the true generalization capabilities of task-generalist robotic policies.

</details>


### [173] [CableTract: A Co-Designed Cable-Driven Field Robot for Low-Compaction, Off-Grid Capable Agriculture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09938)
*Ozgur Yilmaz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Framework integrates mechanics, energy, economics, and environmental impacts for CableTract, a cable-driven agricultural robot with stationary power units and lightweight implement carriage, showing energy and compaction advantages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional tractors waste energy moving heavy bodies; existing feasibility studies lack integrated analysis of mechanics, energy harvest, economics, and environmental impacts in a single reproducible framework.

Method: Developed integrated framework combining catenary cable model, drivetrain efficiency chain, stochastic draft model, solar/wind/battery simulator, polygon coverage planner, compaction model, economic engine with life-cycle CO2, and global sensitivity analysis; applied to CableTract with co-designed implement library.

Result: Framework demonstrates CableTract's energy and compaction advantages over conventional tractors, potential for off-grid operation, and provides comprehensive analysis through operating-envelope sweeps and architectural comparisons.

Conclusion: Integrated framework enables holistic evaluation of novel agricultural vehicles; CableTract shows promise for reducing energy consumption, soil compaction, and enabling sustainable off-grid operations through co-designed cable architecture.

Abstract: Conventional field operations spend most of their energy moving the tractor body, not the implement. Yet feasibility studies for novel agricultural vehicles rarely tie mechanics, energy harvest, draft, field geometry, economics, life-cycle CO2, and uncertainty quantification together on a single reproducible code path. This paper builds such a framework and applies it to CableTract, a two-module cable-driven field robot. A stationary Main Unit (winch + motor + battery + harvester module) (MU) and a lighter Anchor module (held by helical screw piles) tension a cable across a strip while a lightweight implement carriage rolls along it. The heavy bodies stay on the headland; only the carriage enters the field. The carriage runs a 10-implement library co-designed for the cable architecture. This co-design is the paper's central analytical lever. The framework is prototype-free. It chains a catenary cable model, a drivetrain efficiency chain, a stochastic draft model fitted to the co-designed library, an hourly solar + wind + battery simulator on six sites, a polygon coverage planner on a 50-field corpus, a contact-pressure compaction model, a discounted cash-flow economics engine with battery replacement and life-cycle CO2, and a global sensitivity analysis on 20 inputs. An operating-envelope sweep and an architectural-variant comparison close the loop. The full implementation is open source. Applied to the codesigned reference, the framework yields energy, compaction advantages and potential off-grid operation.

</details>


### [174] [GPU-Accelerated Continuous-Time Successive Convexification for Contact-Implicit Legged Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09993)
*Samuel C. Buckner,Purnanand Elango*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ci-SCvx extends continuous-time successive convexification to contact-implicit trajectory optimization using integral cross-complementarity constraints, enabling automatic contact sequence discovery without fine discretization while handling full multibody dynamics with friction and impacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contact-implicit trajectory optimization methods require fine time discretization to capture contact events accurately, which increases computational cost and ties solution quality to grid resolution. There's a need for methods that can automatically discover contact sequences without these limitations.

Method: Extends continuous-time successive convexification (ct-SCvx) to contact-implicit optimization by introducing integral cross-complementarity constraints that prevent missing contact events between discretization nodes. Models full multibody dynamics in maximal coordinates with stick-slip friction and partially elastic impacts. Uses backtracking homotopy scheme within sequential convex programming for complementarity constraints. Implemented in Python with JAX for GPU acceleration and custom canonical-form parser for convex subproblems.

Result: Demonstrated on diverse legged-locomotion tasks. Validated in MuJoCo with Gymnasium HalfCheetah model against MuJoCo MPC baseline, showing physically consistent trajectories with lower energy consumption. Achieves over an order of magnitude faster solve times than existing state-of-the-art SCP implementations.

Conclusion: ci-SCvx provides a scalable, efficient framework for contact-implicit trajectory optimization that eliminates the need for fine discretization while handling complex contact dynamics, enabling real-time applications with superior computational performance.

Abstract: Contact-implicit trajectory optimization (CITO) enables the automatic discovery of contact sequences, but most methods rely on fine time discretization to capture all contact events accurately, which increases problem size and runtime while tying solution quality to grid resolution. We extend the recently proposed sequential convex programming (SCP) approach for trajectory optimization, continuous-time successive convexification (ct-SCvx), to CITO by introducing integral cross-complementarity constraints, which eliminate the risk of missing contact events between discretization nodes while preserving the flexibility of contact mode changes. The resulting framework, contact-implicit successive convexification (ci-SCvx), models full multibody dynamics in maximal coordinates, including stick-slip friction and partially elastic impacts. To handle complementarity constraints, we embed a backtracking homotopy scheme within SCP for reliable convergence. We implement this framework in a stand-alone Python software, leveraging JAX for GPU acceleration and a custom canonical-form parser for the convex subproblems of SCP to avoid the overhead of general-purpose modeling tools such as CVXPY. We demonstrate ci-SCvx on diverse legged-locomotion tasks. In particular, we validate the approach in MuJoCo with the Gymnasium HalfCheetah model against the MuJoCo MPC baseline, showing that a tracking simulation with the optimized torque profiles from ci-SCvx produces physically consistent trajectories with lesser energy consumption. We also show that the resulting software achieves faster solve times than existing state-of-the-art SCP implementations by over an order of magnitude, thereby demonstrating a practically important contribution to scalable real-time trajectory optimization.

</details>


### [175] [Vision-Language-Action Model, Robustness, Multi-modal Learning, Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10055)
*Yuhan Xie,Yuping Yan,Yunqi Zhao,Handing Wang,Yaochu Jin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: STRONG-VLA is a decoupled fine-tuning framework that separates robustness acquisition from task refinement to improve VLA model robustness against multimodal perturbations while maintaining task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are fragile under multimodal perturbations (visual corruption + linguistic noise), and existing robustness approaches cause conflicting optimization between robustness and task fidelity through joint training.

Method: Two-stage decoupled fine-tuning: Stage I exposes model to curriculum of increasing multimodal perturbations for progressive robustness learning; Stage II re-aligns model with clean task distributions to recover execution fidelity while preserving robustness.

Result: Achieves up to 12.60% improvement on OpenVLA under seen perturbations and 7.77% under unseen perturbations; similar gains on OpenVLA-OFT (+14.48%/+13.81%) and pi0 (+16.49%/+5.58%); validated on AIRBOT robotic platform.

Conclusion: Decoupled optimization is crucial for multimodal robustness; STRONG-VLA provides a simple yet principled framework for robust embodied control with strong cross-architecture generalization.

Abstract: Despite their strong performance in embodied tasks, recent Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models remain highly fragile under multimodal perturbations, where visual corruption and linguistic noise jointly induce distribution shifts that degrade task-level execution. Existing robustness approaches typically rely on joint training with perturbed data, treating robustness as a static objective, which leads to conflicting optimization between robustness and task fidelity. In this work, we propose STRONG-VLA, a decoupled fine-tuning framework that explicitly separates robustness acquisition from task-aligned refinement. In Stage I, the model is exposed to a curriculum of multimodal perturbations with increasing difficulty, enabling progressive robustness learning under controlled distribution shifts. In Stage II, the model is re-aligned with clean task distributions to recover execution fidelity while preserving robustness. We further establish a comprehensive benchmark with 28 perturbation types spanning both textual and visual modalities, grounded in realistic sources of sensor noise, occlusion, and instruction corruption. Extensive experiments on the LIBERO benchmark show that STRONG-VLA consistently improves task success rates across multiple VLA architectures. On OpenVLA, our method achieves gains of up to 12.60% under seen perturbations and 7.77% under unseen perturbations. Notably, similar or larger improvements are observed on OpenVLA-OFT (+14.48% / +13.81%) and pi0 (+16.49% / +5.58%), demonstrating strong cross-architecture generalization. Real-world experiments on an AIRBOT robotic platform further validate its practical effectiveness. These results highlight the importance of decoupled optimization for multimodal robustness and establish STRONG-VLA as a simple yet principled framework for robust embodied control.

</details>


### [176] [Natural Gradient Gaussian Approximation Filter on Lie Groups for Robot State Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10057)
*Tianyi Zhang,Wenhan Cao,Chang Liu,Yao Lyu,Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NANO-L filter: A natural gradient-based Gaussian approximation filter for Lie group state estimation that avoids linearization by formulating filtering as optimization over increment variables, achieving 40% lower error than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate state estimation on Lie group manifolds (e.g., for legged robots) is challenged by nonlinear observation models on curved manifolds. Existing filters rely on local linearization in tangent space, leading to accumulated estimation errors.

Method: Reformulate manifold filtering as parameter optimization over Gaussian-distributed increment variables, avoiding linearization. The increment is mapped to Lie group via exponential operator and acts multiplicatively on prior estimate. Propose natural gradient optimization using Fisher information matrix to account for tangent space curvature, resulting in NANO-L filter. For invariant observation models, covariance update admits exact closed-form solution.

Result: Hardware experiments on Unitree GO2 legged robot across different terrains show NANO-L achieves approximately 40% lower estimation error than commonly used filters at comparable computational cost.

Conclusion: NANO-L filter provides superior state estimation accuracy on Lie group manifolds by avoiding linearization through natural gradient optimization, with computational efficiency maintained through closed-form covariance updates for invariant observation models.

Abstract: Accurate state estimation for robotic systems evolving on Lie group manifolds, such as legged robots, is a prerequisite for achieving agile control. However, this task is challenged by nonlinear observation models defined on curved manifolds, where existing filters rely on local linearization in the tangent space to handle such nonlinearity, leading to accumulated estimation errors. To address this limitation, we reformulate manifold filtering as a parameter optimization problem over a Gaussian-distributed increment variable, thereby avoiding linearization. Under this formulation, the increment can be mapped to the Lie group through the exponential operator, where it acts multiplicatively on the prior estimate to yield the posterior state. We further propose a natural gradient optimization scheme for solving this problem, whose iteration process leverages the Fisher information matrix of the increment variable to account for the curvature of the tangent space. This results in an iterative algorithm named the Natural Gradient Gaussian Approximation on Lie Groups (NANO-L) filter. Leveraging the perturbation model in Lie derivative, we prove that for the invariant observation model widely adopted in robotic localization tasks, the covariance update in NANO-L admits an exact closed-form solution, eliminating the need for iterative updates thus improving computational efficiency. Hardware experiments on a Unitree GO2 legged robot operating across different terrains demonstrate that NANO-L achieves approximately 40% lower estimation error than commonly used filters at a comparable computational cost.

</details>


### [177] [A Ray Intersection Algorithm for Fast Growth Distance Computation Between Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10058)
*Akshay Thirugnanam,Koushil Sreenath*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Efficient algorithm for computing growth distance between convex sets using Minkowski difference and iterative polyhedral approximations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growth distance provides unified measure for set intersection/separation, unlike minimum distance. Need efficient computation for robotics applications like motion planning and simulation.

Method: Reduce growth distance to ray intersection on Minkowski difference set. Solve via iterative construction of inner/outer polyhedral approximations with primal/dual feasibility and monotone convergence.

Result: Open-source implementation achieves state-of-the-art performance across various convex sets. Extensive benchmarks demonstrate efficiency.

Conclusion: Algorithm enables efficient growth distance computation with applications in robotics motion planning and rigid-body simulation.

Abstract: In this paper, we discuss an efficient algorithm for computing the growth distance between two compact convex sets with representable support functions. The growth distance between two sets is the minimum scaling factor such that the sets intersect when scaled about some center points. Unlike the minimum distance between sets, the growth distance provides a unified measure for set intersection and separation. We first reduce the growth distance problem to an equivalent ray intersection problem on the Minkowski difference set. Then, we propose an algorithm to solve the ray intersection problem by iteratively constructing inner and outer polyhedral approximations of the Minkowski difference set. We show that our algorithm satisfies several key properties, such as primal and dual feasibility and monotone convergence. We provide extensive benchmark results for our algorithm and show that our open-source implementation achieves state-of-the-art performance across a wide variety of convex sets. Finally, we demonstrate robotics applications of our algorithm in motion planning and rigid-body simulation.

</details>


### [178] [MoRI: Mixture of RL and IL Experts for Long-Horizon Manipulation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10165)
*Yaohang Xu,Lianjie Ma,Gewei Zuo,Wentao Zhang,Han Ding,Lijun Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MoRI: A hybrid system that dynamically switches between IL and RL experts based on action variance to handle coarse movements and fine-grained manipulations, achieving 97.5% success rate with minimal human intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IL suffers from compounding errors and distribution shift, while RL has low sample efficiency and high trial-and-error costs. Existing hybrid methods struggle with complex tasks, necessitating a better approach that leverages both paradigms effectively.

Method: MoRI dynamically switches between IL and RL experts based on the variance of expert actions. It uses offline pre-training followed by online fine-tuning, with IL-based regularization applied to the RL component for safety and minimal human intervention.

Result: Achieves 97.5% average success rate across four complex real-world tasks within 2-5 hours of fine-tuning. Reduces human intervention by 85.8% and shortens convergence time by 21% compared to baseline RL algorithms.

Conclusion: MoRI effectively combines the strengths of IL and RL for robotic manipulation, demonstrating superior performance, reduced human intervention, and faster convergence in complex real-world tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Imitation Learning (IL) are the standard frameworks for policy acquisition in manipulation. While IL offers efficient policy derivation, it suffers from compounding errors and distribution shift. Conversely, RL facilitates autonomous exploration but is frequently hindered by low sample efficiency and the high cost of trial and error. Since existing hybrid methods often struggle with complex tasks, we introduce Mixture of RL and IL Experts (MoRI). This system dynamically switches between IL and RL experts based on the variance of expert actions to handle coarse movements and fine-grained manipulations. MoRI employs an offline pre-training stage followed by online fine-tuning to accelerate convergence. To maintain exploration safety and minimize human intervention, the system applies IL-based regularization to the RL component. Evaluation across four complex real-world tasks shows that MoRI achieves an average success rate of 97.5% within 2 to 5 hours of fine-tuning. Compared to baseline RL algorithms, MoRI reduces human intervention by 85.8% and shortens convergence time by 21%, demonstrating its capability in robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [179] [Device-Conditioned Neural Architecture Search for Efficient Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10170)
*Yiming Wu,Huan Wang,Zhenghao Chen,Ge Yuan,Dong Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DC-QFA is a unified framework for efficient deployment of visuomotor policies across heterogeneous robotic hardware through device-conditioned quantization-aware training and hardware-constrained architecture search, enabling 2-3× acceleration with minimal performance loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing complexity of visuomotor policies poses deployment challenges with heterogeneous robotic hardware constraints. Existing model-efficient approaches are device- and model-specific, lack generalizability, and require time-consuming per-device optimization during adaptation.

Method: Proposes DC-QFA framework with: 1) A single supernet spanning design space over network architectures and mixed-precision bit-widths, optimized with latency- and memory-aware regularization guided by per-device lookup tables; 2) Once-for-all lightweight search for optimal subnet per target platform without re-optimization; 3) Multi-step on-policy distillation to mitigate error accumulation during closed-loop execution under low precision.

Result: Achieves 2-3× acceleration on edge devices, consumer-grade GPUs, and cloud platforms with negligible performance drop in task success. Real-world evaluations on Inovo robot with force/torque sensor validate stable, contact-rich manipulation even under severe quantization.

Conclusion: DC-QFA provides a unified solution for efficient deployment of visuomotor policies across heterogeneous hardware, substantially reducing deployment time while maintaining performance through device-conditioned quantization-aware training and hardware-constrained architecture search.

Abstract: The growing complexity of visuomotor policies poses significant challenges for deployment with heterogeneous robotic hardware constraints. However, most existing model-efficient approaches for robotic manipulation are device- and model-specific, lack generalizability, and require time-consuming per-device optimization during the adaptation process. In this work, we propose a unified framework named \textbf{D}evice-\textbf{C}onditioned \textbf{Q}uantization-\textbf{F}or-\textbf{A}ll (DC-QFA) which amortizes deployment effort with the device-conditioned quantization-aware training and hardware-constrained architecture search. Specifically, we introduce a single supernet that spans a rich design space over network architectures and mixed-precision bit-widths. It is optimized with latency- and memory-aware regularization, guided by per-device lookup tables. With this supernet, for each target platform, we can perform a once-for-all lightweight search to select an optimal subnet without any per-device re-optimization, which enables more generalizable deployment across heterogeneous hardware, and substantially reduces deployment time. To improve long-horizon stability under low precision, we further introduce multi-step on-policy distillation to mitigate error accumulation during closed-loop execution. Extensive experiments on three representative policy backbones, such as DiffusionPolicy-T, MDT-V, and OpenVLA-OFT, demonstrate that our DC-QFA achieves $2\text{-}3\times$ acceleration on edge devices, consumer-grade GPUs, and cloud platforms, with negligible performance drop in task success. Real-world evaluations on an Inovo robot equipped with a force/torque sensor further validates that our low-bit DC-QFA policies maintain stable, contact-rich manipulation even under severe quantization.

</details>


### [180] [ReaLiTy and LADS: A Unified Framework and Dataset Suite for LiDAR Adaptation Across Sensors and Adverse Weather Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10213)
*Vivek Anand,Bharat Lohani,Rakesh Mishra,Gaurav Pandey*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ReaLiTy is a physics-informed framework for transforming LiDAR data across sensors and weather conditions, accompanied by LADS dataset for reproducible domain adaptation research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LiDAR datasets lack physically consistent observations of the same scene under varying sensor configurations and weather conditions, limiting systematic analysis of domain shifts and robustness evaluation for perception systems.

Method: A unified physics-informed framework integrating physically grounded cues with learning-based modules for realistic intensity patterns, plus physics-based weather models for geometric and radiometric degradations. The LiDAR Adaptation Dataset Suite (LADS) provides transformation-ready point clouds with one-to-one correspondence to original datasets.

Result: Experiments demonstrate improved cross-domain consistency and realistic weather effects, providing a reproducible foundation for studying LiDAR adaptation and simulation-driven perception.

Conclusion: ReaLiTy and LADS address the critical need for physically consistent domain adaptation in LiDAR perception, enabling systematic study of sensor and weather variations for robust intelligent transportation systems.

Abstract: Reliable LiDAR perception requires robustness across sensors, environments, and adverse weather. However, existing datasets rarely provide physically consistent observations of the same scene under varying sensor configurations and weather conditions, limiting systematic analysis of domain shifts. This work presents ReaLiTy, a unified physics-informed framework that transforms LiDAR data to match target sensor specifications and weather conditions. The framework integrates physically grounded cues with a learning-based module to generate realistic intensity patterns, while a physics-based weather model introduces consistent geometric and radiometric degradations. Building on this framework, we introduce the LiDAR Adaptation Dataset Suite (LADS), a collection of physically consistent, transformation-ready point clouds with one-to-one correspondence to original datasets. Experiments demonstrate improved cross-domain consistency and realistic weather effects. ReaLiTy and LADS provide a reproducible foundation for studying LiDAR adaptation and simulation-driven perception in intelligent transportation systems.

</details>


### [181] [A Coordinate-Invariant Local Representation of Motion and Force Trajectories for Identification and Generalization Across Coordinate Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10241)
*Arno Verduyn,Erwin Aertbeliën,Maxim Vochten,Joris De Schutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DUTIR: A novel Dual-Upper-Triangular Invariant Representation for rigid-body and interaction-force trajectories with improved robustness to singularities and measurement noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory analysis for rigid bodies and interaction forces is essential in robotics and biomechanics, but existing coordinate-invariant representations suffer from sensitivity to measurement noise and singularity issues where representations are not uniquely defined.

Method: Introduces Dual-Upper-Triangular Invariant Representation (DUTIR), a novel coordinate-invariant representation formulated at an abstract level applicable to both rigid-body and interaction-force trajectories, along with its computational algorithm.

Result: DUTIR provides improved robustness to singularities compared to existing coordinate-invariant representations, making it more reliable for trajectory segmentation, recognition, and prediction tasks across different coordinate systems.

Conclusion: DUTIR offers a versatile tool for robotics and biomechanics applications by addressing key limitations of existing coordinate-invariant trajectory representations through improved singularity robustness and broader applicability.

Abstract: Identifying the trajectories of rigid bodies and of interaction forces is essential for a wide range of tasks in robotics, biomechanics, and related domains. These tasks include trajectory segmentation, recognition, and prediction. For these tasks, a key challenge lies in achieving consistent results when the trajectory is expressed in different coordinate systems. A way to address this challenge is to utilize trajectory models that can generalize across coordinate systems. The focus of this paper is on such trajectory models obtained by transforming the trajectory into a coordinate-invariant representation. However, coordinate-invariant representations often suffer from sensitivity to measurement noise and the manifestation of singularities in the representation, where the representation is not uniquely defined. This paper aims to address this limitation by introducing the novel Dual-Upper-Triangular Invariant Representation (DUTIR), with improved robustness to singularities, along with its computational algorithm. The proposed representation is formulated at a level of abstraction that makes it applicable to both rigid-body trajectories and interaction-force trajectories, hence making it a versatile tool for robotics, biomechanics, and related domains.

</details>


### [182] [Trajectory-based actuator identification via differentiable simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10351)
*Vyacheslav Kovalev,Ekaterina Chaikovskaia,Egor Davydenko,Roman Gorbachev*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Differentiable simulation enables actuator model identification from encoder motion alone, achieving better trajectory alignment than supervised baseline and improving downstream locomotion performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate actuator models are essential for simulation-to-reality transfer, but obtaining high-fidelity dynamics typically requires dedicated test stands and torque sensing, which is expensive and impractical.

Method: Trajectory-based actuator identification using differentiable simulation to fit system-level actuator models from encoder motion alone, posed as a trajectory-matching problem that optimizes actuator and simulator parameters through backpropagation without torque sensors or motor-control internals.

Result: Achieved much tighter trajectory alignment than supervised stand-trained baseline, reducing mean absolute position error from 14.20 mrad to as low as 7.54 mrad (1.88× improvement). Downstream locomotion study showed 46% increased travel distance and 75% reduced rotational deviation.

Conclusion: Differentiable simulation enables effective actuator identification from minimal sensor data (encoder motion alone), improving simulation fidelity and downstream task performance without requiring expensive torque sensing or dedicated test stands.

Abstract: Accurate actuation models are critical for bridging the gap between simulation and real robot behavior, yet obtaining high-fidelity actuator dynamics typically requires dedicated test stands and torque sensing. We present a trajectory-based actuator identification method that uses differentiable simulation to fit system-level actuator models from encoder motion alone. Identification is posed as a trajectory-matching problem: given commanded joint positions and measured joint angles and velocities, we optimize actuator and simulator parameters by backpropagating through the simulator, without torque sensors, current/voltage measurements, or access to embedded motor-control internals. The framework supports multiple model classes, ranging from compact structured parameterizations to neural actuator mappings, within a unified optimization pipeline.
