On held-out real-robot trajectories under identical commands, the proposed torque-sensor-free identification achieves much tighter trajectory alignment than a supervised stand-trained baseline dominated by steady-state data, reducing mean absolute position error from 14.20 mrad to as low as 7.54 mrad (1.88 times). Finally, we demonstrate downstream impact in a real-robot locomotion study: training policies with the refined actuator model increases travel distance by 46% and reduces rotational deviation by 75% relative to the baseline.

</details>


### [183] [AnySlot: Goal-Conditioned Vision-Language-Action Policies for Zero-Shot Slot-Level Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10432)
*Zhaofeng Hu,Sifan Zhou,Qinbo Zhang,Rongtao Xu,Qi Su,Ci-Jyun Liang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AnySlot introduces a hierarchical framework for precise object placement in robotics by generating explicit visual goals from language instructions, outperforming flat VLA baselines on the new SlotBench benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current monolithic Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies struggle with precise object placement under compositional language instructions, particularly for slot-level tasks requiring both reliable slot grounding and sub-centimeter execution accuracy.

Method: AnySlot reduces compositional complexity by introducing an explicit spatial visual goal as intermediate representation between language grounding and control. It first generates a scene marker (visual goal) from language, then executes this goal with a goal-conditioned VLA policy, decoupling high-level slot selection from low-level execution.

Result: AnySlot significantly outperforms flat VLA baselines and previous modular grounding methods in zero-shot slot-level placement. The paper also introduces SlotBench, a comprehensive simulation benchmark with nine task categories for evaluating structured spatial reasoning in slot-level placement.

Conclusion: The hierarchical design of AnySlot effectively addresses the precision challenges in slot-level placement tasks by separating semantic grounding from spatial execution, providing both semantic accuracy and spatial robustness for compositional robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies have emerged as a versatile paradigm for generalist robotic manipulation. However, precise object placement under compositional language instructions remains a major challenge for modern monolithic VLA policies. Slot-level tasks require both reliable slot grounding and sub-centimeter execution accuracy. To this end, we propose AnySlot, a framework that reduces compositional complexity by introducing an explicit spatial visual goal as an intermediate representation between language grounding and control. AnySlot turns language into an explicit visual goal by generating a scene marker, then executes this goal with a goal-conditioned VLA policy. This hierarchical design effectively decouples high-level slot selection from low-level execution, ensuring both semantic accuracy and spatial robustness. Furthermore, recognizing the lack of existing benchmarks for such precision-demanding tasks, we introduce SlotBench, a comprehensive simulation benchmark featuring nine task categories tailored to evaluate structured spatial reasoning in slot-level placement. Extensive experiments show that AnySlot significantly outperforms flat VLA baselines and previous modular grounding methods in zero-shot slot-level placement.

</details>


### [184] [PRoID: Predicted Rate of Information Delivery in Multi-Robot Exploration and Relaying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10433)
*Seungchan Kim,Seungjae Baek,Micah Corah,Graeme Best,Brady Moon,Sebastian Scherer*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PRoID is a novel relay criterion for multi-robot exploration that uses learned map prediction to dynamically decide when robots should stop exploring and return to base, outperforming fixed-schedule strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-robot exploration systems need to not only explore but also deliver information to a base station within time limits. Existing approaches either ignore reporting requirements or use fixed relay schedules that cannot adapt to environment structure, team composition, or mission progress.

Method: PRoID (Predicted Rate of Information Delivery) uses learned map prediction to estimate each robot's future information gain along its planned path, accounting for what teammates are already relaying. It triggers relay when immediate return yields higher information delivery per unit time. PRoID-Safe extends this by incorporating robot survival probability into the relay criterion.

Result: Evaluation on real-world indoor floor plan datasets shows that PRoID and PRoID-Safe outperform fixed-schedule baselines, with stronger relative gains in failure scenarios.

Conclusion: PRoID provides an adaptive relay strategy that dynamically balances exploration and information delivery based on predicted information gain and team coordination, with PRoID-Safe adding robustness to failure risks.

Abstract: We address Multi-Robot Exploration and Relaying (MRER): a team of robots must explore an unknown environment and deliver acquired information to a fixed base station within a mission time limit. The central challenge is deciding when each robot should stop exploring and relay: this depends on what the robot is likely to find ahead, what information it uniquely holds, and whether immediate or future delivery is more valuable. Prior approaches either ignore the reporting requirement entirely or rely on fixed-schedule relay strategies that cannot adapt to environment structure, team composition, or mission progress. We introduce PRoID (Predicted Rate of Information Delivery), a relay criterion that uses learned map prediction to estimate each robot's future information gain along its planned path, accounting for what teammates are already relaying. PRoID triggers relay when immediate return yields higher information delivery per unit time. We further propose PRoID-Safe, a failure-aware extension that incorporates robot survival probability into the relay criterion, naturally biasing decisions toward earlier relay as failure risk grows. We evaluate on real-world indoor floor plan datasets and show that PRoID and PRoID-Safe outperform fixed-schedule baselines, with stronger relative gains in failure scenarios.

</details>


### [185] [VLN-NF: Feasibility-Aware Vision-and-Language Navigation with False-Premise Instructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10533)
*Hung-Ting Su,Ting-Jun Wang,Jia-Fong Yeh,Min Sun,Winston H. Hsu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLN-NF introduces a new benchmark for Vision-and-Language Navigation with false-premise instructions where targets are absent, requiring agents to explore, gather evidence, and explicitly output NOT-FOUND.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional VLN benchmarks assume instructions are feasible and targets exist, leaving agents ill-equipped to handle false-premise goals where the referenced target is absent from the specified room.

Method: VLN-NF is constructed via a scalable pipeline using LLM to rewrite VLN instructions and VLM to verify target absence. The paper proposes REV-SPL for joint evaluation and ROAM, a two-stage hybrid approach combining supervised room-level navigation with LLM/VLM-driven in-room exploration guided by free-space clearance prior.

Result: ROAM achieves the best REV-SPL among compared methods, while baselines often under-explore and terminate prematurely under unreliable instructions.

Conclusion: VLN-NF addresses a critical gap in VLN evaluation by introducing false-premise instructions, and ROAM demonstrates effective handling of such challenging scenarios through evidence-gathering exploration.

Abstract: Conventional Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) benchmarks assume instructions are feasible and the referenced target exists, leaving agents ill-equipped to handle false-premise goals. We introduce VLN-NF, a benchmark with false-premise instructions where the target is absent from the specified room and agents must navigate, gather evidence through in-room exploration, and explicitly output NOT-FOUND. VLN-NF is constructed via a scalable pipeline that rewrites VLN instructions using an LLM and verifies target absence with a VLM, producing plausible yet factually incorrect goals. We further propose REV-SPL to jointly evaluate room reaching, exploration coverage, and decision correctness. To address this challenge, we present ROAM, a two-stage hybrid that combines supervised room-level navigation with LLM/VLM-driven in-room exploration guided by a free-space clearance prior. ROAM achieves the best REV-SPL among compared methods, while baselines often under-explore and terminate prematurely under unreliable instructions. VLN-NF project page can be found at https://vln-nf.github.io/.

</details>


### [186] [Simple but Stable, Fast and Safe: Achieve End-to-end Control by High-Fidelity Differentiable Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10548)
*Fanxing Li,Shengyang Wang,Yuxiang Huang,Fangyu Sun,Yufei Yan,Danping Zou,Wenxian Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: End-to-end reinforcement learning policy maps depth images directly to low-level bodyrate commands for quadrotor obstacle avoidance, achieving high-speed flight without traditional planning pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing obstacle avoidance approaches treat quadrotors as point-mass models, generating trajectories that become dynamically infeasible at high speeds, exceeding controller capabilities. There's a need for direct low-level control that avoids dynamic infeasibility issues.

Method: Uses reinforcement learning via differentiable simulation to train an end-to-end policy that directly maps depth images to low-level bodyrate commands. Employs high-fidelity simulation with parameter identification to reduce sim-to-real gaps. Uses differentiable simulation for accurate gradient computation without expert guidance. The policy has a lightweight inference pipeline without explicit mapping, backbone networks, primitives, recurrent structures, or backend controllers.

Result: Achieves highest success rate and lowest jerk among state-of-the-art baselines across multiple benchmarks. Demonstrates strong generalization with zero-shot deployment in unseen outdoor environments, reaching speeds up to 7.5m/s and stable flight in super-dense forests.

Conclusion: The proposed end-to-end approach successfully addresses dynamic infeasibility issues in high-speed quadrotor obstacle avoidance by directly mapping perception to low-level control, enabling full flight envelope control without traditional planning pipelines.

Abstract: Obstacle avoidance is a fundamental vision-based task essential for enabling quadrotors to perform advanced applications. When planning the trajectory, existing approaches both on optimization and learning typically regard quadrotor as a point-mass model, giving path or velocity commands then tracking the commands by outer-loop controller. However, at high speeds, planned trajectories sometimes become dynamically infeasible in actual flight, which beyond the capacity of controller. In this paper, we propose a novel end-to-end policy that directly maps depth images to low-level bodyrate commands by reinforcement learning via differentiable simulation. The high-fidelity simulation in training after parameter identification significantly reduces all the gaps between training, simulation and real world. Analytical process by differentiable simulation provides accurate gradient to ensure efficiently training the low-level policy without expert guidance. The policy employs a lightweight and the most simple inference pipeline that runs without explicit mapping, backbone networks, primitives, recurrent structures, or backend controllers, nor curriculum or privileged guidance. By inferring low-level command directly to the hardware controller, the method enables full flight envelope control and avoids the dynamic-infeasible issue.Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves the highest success rate and the lowest jerk among state-of-the-art baselines across multiple benchmarks. The policy also exhibits strong generalization, successfully deploying zero-shot in unseen, outdoor environments while reaching speeds of up to 7.5m/s as well as stably flying in the super-dense forest.

</details>


### [187] [AffordGen: Generating Diverse Demonstrations for Generalizable Object Manipulation with Afford Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10579)
*Jiawei Zhang,Kaizhe Hu,Yingqian Huang,Yuanchen Ju,Zhengrong Xue,Huazhe Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AffordGen uses 3D generative models and vision foundation models to create affordance-aware datasets from semantic keypoint correspondences across 3D meshes, enabling robust visuomotor policies with zero-shot generalization to unseen objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern imitation learning methods for robot manipulation are constrained by geometric variations due to limited data diversity, limiting their generalization capabilities to unseen objects.

Method: AffordGen framework leverages 3D generative models and vision foundation models to extract semantic correspondence of meaningful keypoints across large-scale 3D meshes, generating new robot manipulation trajectories to create a large-scale, affordance-aware dataset for training closed-loop visuomotor policies.

Result: Policies trained with AffordGen achieve high success rates in simulation and real-world experiments, enabling zero-shot generalization to truly unseen objects and significantly improving data efficiency in robot learning.

Conclusion: AffordGen successfully combines the semantic generalizability of affordances with the reactive robustness of end-to-end learning, overcoming limitations of data diversity in imitation learning for robot manipulation.

Abstract: Despite the recent success of modern imitation learning methods in robot manipulation, their performance is often constrained by geometric variations due to limited data diversity. Leveraging powerful 3D generative models and vision foundation models (VFMs), the proposed AffordGen framework overcomes this limitation by utilizing the semantic correspondence of meaningful keypoints across large-scale 3D meshes to generate new robot manipulation trajectories. This large-scale, affordance-aware dataset is then used to train a robust, closed-loop visuomotor policy, combining the semantic generalizability of affordances with the reactive robustness of end-to-end learning. Experiments in simulation and the real world show that policies trained with AffordGen achieve high success rates and enable zero-shot generalization to truly unseen objects, significantly improving data efficiency in robot learning.

</details>


### [188] [MonoEM-GS: Monocular Expectation-Maximization Gaussian Splatting SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10593)
*Evgenii Kruzhkov,Sven Behnke*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MonoEM-GS is a monocular mapping pipeline that integrates noisy geometric predictions from foundation models into a global Gaussian Splatting representation using Expectation-Maximization and ICP alignment, with multi-modal features for open-set segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feed-forward geometric foundation models provide dense point clouds and camera motion priors for monocular SLAM, but their predictions are view-dependent, noisy, and inconsistent across viewpoints and image transformations, requiring stabilization and consistency handling.

Method: Integrates geometric predictions into global Gaussian Splatting representation using Expectation-Maximization formulation to stabilize geometry, employs ICP-based alignment for monocular pose estimation, and parameterizes Gaussians with multi-modal features for downstream tasks.

Result: Evaluated on 7-Scenes, TUM RGB-D and Replica datasets, showing improved performance compared to recent baselines in monocular mapping with geometric consistency and open-set segmentation capabilities.

Conclusion: MonoEM-GS successfully addresses view-dependent inconsistencies in geometric foundation model predictions through Gaussian Splatting with EM stabilization, enabling robust monocular mapping with multi-modal feature representation for downstream applications.

Abstract: Feed-forward geometric foundation models can infer dense point clouds and camera motion directly from RGB streams, providing priors for monocular SLAM. However, their predictions are often view-dependent and noisy: geometry can vary across viewpoints and under image transformations, and local metric properties may drift between frames. We present MonoEM-GS, a monocular mapping pipeline that integrates such geometric predictions into a global Gaussian Splatting representation while explicitly addressing these inconsistencies. MonoEM-GS couples Gaussian Splatting with an Expectation--Maximization formulation to stabilize geometry, and employs ICP-based alignment for monocular pose estimation. Beyond geometry, MonoEM-GS parameterizes Gaussians with multi-modal features, enabling in-place open-set segmentation and other downstream queries directly on the reconstructed map. We evaluate MonoEM-GS on 7-Scenes, TUM RGB-D and Replica, and compare against recent baselines.

</details>


### [189] [AWARE: Adaptive Whole-body Active Rotating Control for Enhanced LiDAR-Inertial Odometry under Human-in-the-Loop Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10598)
*Yizhe Zhang,Jianping Li,Liangliang Yin,Zhen Dong,Bisheng Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AWARE is a bio-inspired active yawing framework that uses UAV rotational agility to improve LiDAR odometry observability in feature-sparse environments, combining MPC with RL for adaptive control while maintaining safe human-in-the-loop operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In HITL UAV surveying, narrow-FOV LiDAR sensors struggle in geometrically degenerate scenes, causing LIO drift and unstable state estimation that compromises safety and surveying reliability. Current systems lack adaptive mechanisms to improve observability without additional hardware.

Method: AWARE uses a differentiable MPC framework embedded in an RL loop: MPC identifies optimal yaw directions for information gain, while an RL agent adaptively adjusts MPC cost weights based on environmental context. A Safe Flight Corridor decouples operator navigation from autonomous yaw optimization.

Result: Extensive experiments in diverse simulated and real-world environments demonstrate improved LIO observability and state estimation accuracy through active yawing, enabling more reliable HITL operation in feature-sparse conditions.

Conclusion: AWARE successfully addresses LIO observability limitations in resource-constrained UAVs by leveraging rotational agility for active sensing, providing an adaptive control framework that maintains safe HITL operation while improving surveying reliability.

Abstract: Human-in-the-loop (HITL) UAV operation is essential in complex and safety-critical aerial surveying environments, where human operators provide navigation intent while onboard autonomy must maintain accurate and robust state estimation. A key challenge in this setting is that resource-constrained UAV platforms are often limited to narrow-field-of-view LiDAR sensors. In geometrically degenerate or feature-sparse scenes, limited sensing coverage often weakens LiDAR Inertial Odometry (LIO)'s observability, causing drift accumulation, degraded geometric accuracy, and unstable state estimation, which directly compromise safe and effective HITL operation and the reliability of downstream surveying products. To overcome this limitation, we present AWARE, a bio-inspired whole-body active yawing framework that exploits the UAV's own rotational agility to extend the effective sensor horizon and improve LIO's observability without additional mechanical actuation. The core of AWARE is a differentiable Model Predictive Control (MPC) framework embedded in a Reinforcement Learning (RL) loop. It first identifies the viewing direction that maximizes information gain across the full yaw space, and a lightweight RL agent then adjusts the MPC cost weights online according to the current environmental context, enabling an adaptive balance between estimation accuracy and flight stability. A Safe Flight Corridor mechanism further ensures operational safety within this HITL paradigm by decoupling the operator's navigational intent from autonomous yaw optimization to enable safe and efficient cooperative control. We validate AWARE through extensive experiments in diverse simulated and real-world environments.

</details>


### [190] [OmniUMI: Towards Physically Grounded Robot Learning via Human-Aligned Multimodal Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10647)
*Shaqi Luo,Yuanyuan Li,Youhao Hu,Chenhao Yu,Chaoran Xu,Jiachen Zhang,Guocai Yao,Tiejun Huang,Ran He,Zhongyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OmniUMI is a unified framework for contact-rich robot learning that captures multimodal physical interaction signals (RGB, depth, tactile, internal/external forces) in a handheld system with human-aligned demonstration and deployment consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UMI-style interfaces are largely visuomotor and lack access to crucial physical interaction signals needed for contact-rich manipulation tasks, where success depends on tactile interaction, internal grasping force, and external interaction wrench that are difficult to infer from vision alone.

Method: OmniUMI synchronously captures RGB, depth, trajectory, tactile sensing, internal grasping force, and external interaction wrench within a compact handheld system with shared embodiment design. It provides dual-force feedback through bilateral gripper feedback and natural perception of external interaction wrench. The framework extends diffusion policy with multimodal observations and deploys learned policies through impedance-based execution.

Result: Experiments demonstrate reliable sensing and strong downstream performance on force-sensitive pick-and-place, interactive surface erasing, and tactile-informed selective release tasks.

Conclusion: OmniUMI combines physically grounded multimodal data acquisition with human-aligned interaction, providing a scalable foundation for learning contact-rich manipulation by addressing the limitations of vision-only systems.

Abstract: UMI-style interfaces enable scalable robot learning, but existing systems remain largely visuomotor, relying primarily on RGB observations and trajectory while providing only limited access to physical interaction signals. This becomes a fundamental limitation in contact-rich manipulation, where success depends on contact dynamics such as tactile interaction, internal grasping force, and external interaction wrench that are difficult to infer from vision alone. We present OmniUMI, a unified framework for physically grounded robot learning via human-aligned multimodal interaction. OmniUMI synchronously captures RGB, depth, trajectory, tactile sensing, internal grasping force, and external interaction wrench within a compact handheld system, while maintaining collection--deployment consistency through a shared embodiment design. To support human-aligned demonstration, OmniUMI provides dual-force feedback through bilateral gripper feedback and natural perception of external interaction wrench in the handheld embodiment. Built on this interface, we extend diffusion policy with visual, tactile, and force-related observations, and deploy the learned policy through impedance-based execution for unified regulation of motion and contact behavior. Experiments demonstrate reliable sensing and strong downstream performance on force-sensitive pick-and-place, interactive surface erasing, and tactile-informed selective release. Overall, OmniUMI combines physically grounded multimodal data acquisition with human-aligned interaction, providing a scalable foundation for learning contact-rich manipulation.

</details>


### [191] [LIDEA: Human-to-Robot Imitation Learning via Implicit Feature Distillation and Explicit Geometry Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10677)
*Yifu Xu,Bokai Lin,Xinyu Zhan,Hongjie Fang,Yong-Lu Li,Cewu Lu,Lixin Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LIDEA framework bridges human-robot embodiment gap using implicit feature distillation and explicit geometric alignment to leverage human videos for robot policy learning, reducing need for robot demonstrations by 80%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot learning is limited by scarce robotic demonstrations, while human videos offer abundant interaction data. The key challenge is bridging the embodiment gap between human hands and robot arms, as existing visual editing methods introduce artifacts due to appearance and geometry discrepancies.

Method: LIDEA uses a dual-stage transitive distillation pipeline in 2D visual domain to align human and robot representations in shared latent space. In 3D geometric domain, it employs embodiment-agnostic alignment that explicitly decouples embodiment from interaction geometry for consistent 3D-aware perception.

Result: Human data can substitute up to 80% of costly robot demonstrations. The framework successfully transfers unseen patterns from human videos for out-of-distribution generalization, demonstrating both data efficiency and OOD robustness.

Conclusion: LIDEA effectively bridges the human-robot embodiment gap through combined implicit feature distillation and explicit geometric alignment, enabling efficient utilization of human videos for robot policy learning with reduced reliance on robot demonstrations.

Abstract: Scaling up robot learning is hindered by the scarcity of robotic demonstrations, whereas human videos offer a vast, untapped source of interaction data. However, bridging the embodiment gap between human hands and robot arms remains a critical challenge. Existing cross-embodiment transfer strategies typically rely on visual editing, but they often introduce visual artifacts due to intrinsic discrepancies in visual appearance and 3D geometry. To address these limitations, we introduce LIDEA (Implicit Feature Distillation and Explicit Geometric Alignment), an imitation learning framework in which policy learning benefits from human demonstrations. In the 2D visual domain, LIDEA employs a dual-stage transitive distillation pipeline that aligns human and robot representations in a shared latent space. In the 3D geometric domain, we propose an embodiment-agnostic alignment strategy that explicitly decouples embodiment from interaction geometry, ensuring consistent 3D-aware perception. Extensive experiments empirically validate LIDEA from two perspectives: data efficiency and OOD robustness. Results show that human data substitutes up to 80% of costly robot demonstrations, and the framework successfully transfers unseen patterns from human videos for out-of-distribution generalization.

</details>


### [192] [WARPED: Wrist-Aligned Rendering for Robot Policy Learning from Egocentric Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10809)
*Harry Freeman,Chung Hee Kim,George Kantor*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WARPED synthesizes realistic wrist-view observations from human demonstration videos using monocular RGB data to train visuomotor policies, achieving comparable success rates to teleoperated data with 5-8x less collection time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning-from-demonstration approaches rely on multiview camera setups, depth sensors, or custom hardware, limiting them to third-person or egocentric cameras. There's a need for more accessible methods using only monocular RGB data.

Method: Uses egocentric RGB camera data, vision foundation models to initialize interactive scenes, hand-object interaction pipeline for tracking and retargeting to robotic end-effector, and Gaussian Splatting for photo-realistic wrist-view observation synthesis.

Result: Achieves success rates comparable to policies trained on teleoperated demonstration data for five tabletop manipulation tasks while requiring 5-8x less data collection time.

Conclusion: WARPED provides an effective framework for training visuomotor policies using only monocular RGB human demonstration videos, significantly reducing data collection requirements while maintaining performance comparable to traditional teleoperation methods.

Abstract: Recent advancements in learning from human demonstration have shown promising results in addressing the scalability and high cost of data collection required to train robust visuomotor policies. However, existing approaches are often constrained by a reliance on multiview camera setups, depth sensors, or custom hardware and are typically limited to policy execution from third-person or egocentric cameras. In this paper, we present WARPED, a framework designed to synthesize realistic wrist-view observations from human demonstration videos to facilitate the training of visuomotor policies using only monocular RGB data. With data collected from an egocentric RGB camera, our system leverages vision foundation models to initialize the interactive scene. A hand-object interaction pipeline is then employed to track the hand and manipulated object and retarget the trajectories to a robotic end-effector. Lastly, photo-realistic wrist-view observations are synthesized via Gaussian Splatting to directly train a robotic policy. We demonstrate that WARPED achieves success rates comparable to policies trained on teleoperated demonstration data for five tabletop manipulation tasks, while requiring 5-8x less data collection time.

</details>


### [193] [BridgeSim: Unveiling the OL-CL Gap in End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10856)
*Seth Z. Zhao,Luobin Wang,Hongwei Ruan,Yuxin Bao,Yilan Chen,Ziyang Leng,Abhijit Ravichandran,Honglin He,Zewei Zhou,Xu Han,Abhishek Peri,Zhiyu Huang,Pranav Desai,Henrik Christensen,Jiaqi Ma,Bolei Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper identifies root causes of the open-loop to closed-loop performance gap in policy deployment and proposes a test-time adaptation framework to bridge this gap by addressing observational domain shift, objective mismatch, and biased Q-value estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-loop pretrained policies often score high in open-loop evaluations but fail to transfer effectively to closed-loop deployment, creating a significant performance gap (OL-CL gap) that needs to be understood and addressed.

Method: The authors propose a Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) framework that calibrates observational shift, reduces state-action biases, and enforces temporal consistency to address the structural limitations of OL policies in CL deployment.

Result: Extensive experiments show that TTA effectively mitigates planning biases and yields superior scaling dynamics compared to baseline counterparts, while also revealing blind spots in standard OL evaluation protocols.

Conclusion: The primary OL-CL gap stems from objective mismatch creating structural inability to model complex reactive behaviors, which can be addressed through test-time adaptation that corrects biased Q-value estimation and improves temporal awareness.

Abstract: Open-loop (OL) to closed-loop (CL) gap (OL-CL gap) exists when OL-pretrained policies scoring high in OL evaluations fail to transfer effectively in closed-loop (CL) deployment. In this paper, we unveil the root causes of this systemic failure and propose a practical remedy. Specifically, we demonstrate that OL policies suffer from Observational Domain Shift and Objective Mismatch. We show that while the former is largely recoverable with adaptation techniques, the latter creates a structural inability to model complex reactive behaviors, which forms the primary OL-CL gap. We find that a wide range of OL policies learn a biased Q-value estimator that neglects both the reactive nature of CL simulations and the temporal awareness needed to reduce compounding errors. To this end, we propose a Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) framework that calibrates observational shift, reduces state-action biases, and enforces temporal consistency. Extensive experiments show that TTA effectively mitigates planning biases and yields superior scaling dynamics than its baseline counterparts. Furthermore, our analysis highlights the existence of blind spots in standard OL evaluation protocols that fail to capture the realities of closed-loop deployment.

</details>


### [194] [HECTOR: Human-centric Hierarchical Coordination and Supervision of Robotic Fleets under Continual Temporal Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10892)
*Shen Wang,Yinhang Luo,Jie Li,Meng Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HECTOR is a hierarchical human-centric coordination framework for large-scale robotic fleets that enables efficient operator supervision through multimodal interaction, rolling task assignment, and dynamic team coordination under uncertain temporal tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct control of each robot in large fleets is impractical, requiring both fleet autonomy and effective online operator interaction for dynamic environments where tasks need to be added, canceled, prioritized, or modified.

Method: Three-layer hierarchical framework: (I) bidirectional multimodal human-fleet interaction protocol, (II) rolling assignment of tasks to teams within planning horizons, (III) dynamic coordination within teams during execution for detected subtasks.

Result: Extensive human-in-the-loop simulations demonstrate the framework's effectiveness with heterogeneous fleets under various temporal tasks and environmental uncertainties, showing improved computational efficiency and reduced human effort.

Conclusion: HECTOR provides a comprehensive solution for human-centric supervision of large-scale robotic fleets, enabling efficient operator interaction at different granularities while handling uncertain temporal tasks in dynamic environments.

Abstract: Robotic fleets can be extremely efficient when working concurrently and collaboratively, e.g., for delivery, surveillance, search and rescue. However, it can be demanding or even impractical for an operator to directly control each robot. Thus, autonomy of the fleet and its online interaction with the operator are both essential, particularly in dynamic and partially unknown environments. The operator might need to add new tasks, cancel some tasks, change priorities and modify planning results. How to design the procedure for these interactions and efficient algorithms to fulfill these needs have been mostly neglected in the related literature. Thus, this work proposes a human-centric coordination and supervision scheme (HECTOR) for large-scale robotic fleets under continual and uncertain temporal tasks. It consists of three hierarchical layers: (I) the bidirectional and multimodal protocol of online human-fleet interaction, where the operator interacts with and supervises the whole fleet; (II) the rolling assignment of currently-known tasks to teams within a certain horizon, and (III) the dynamic coordination within a team given the detected subtasks during online execution. The overall mission can be as general as temporal logic formulas over collaborative actions. Such hierarchical structure allows human interaction and supervision at different granularities and triggering conditions, to both improve computational efficiency and reduce human effort. Extensive human-in-the-loop simulations are performed over heterogeneous fleets under various temporal tasks and environmental uncertainties.

</details>


### [195] [Ro-SLM: Onboard Small Language Models for Robot Task Planning and Operation Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10929)
*Wenhao Wang,Yanyan Li,Long Jiao,Jiawei Yuan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Ro-SLM is a framework that enables reliable small language model (SLM) deployment on robots by distilling knowledge from large language models (LLMs), allowing onboard operation without cloud dependency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-enabled robots rely on cloud-based models or high-performance computing, limiting deployment in unreliable internet environments or on resource-constrained robots like UAVs and small ground vehicles. Fine-tuned SLMs that support onboard deployment offer a promising alternative.

Method: Ro-SLM uses LLMs to: 1) generate diverse task instructions, 2) produce corresponding ground truth code with minimal human assistance, 3) augment instructions into real-world application scenarios. The framework then fine-tunes SLMs with this dataset, using LLM as a reward function to guide training.

Result: Extensive experiments on UAV operation tasks demonstrate that Ro-SLM improves SLM performance from being incapable of supporting robotic task planning and code generation to achieving performance that approaches LLM levels.

Conclusion: Ro-SLM provides a framework for reliable SLM-driven robot operation by distilling LLM knowledge and reasoning, enabling onboard deployment without cloud dependency for resource-constrained robotic platforms.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) provide robots with contextual reasoning abilities to comprehend human instructions. Yet, current LLM-enabled robots typically depend on cloud-based models or high-performance computing infrastructure, which limit their deployment on robots under unreliable internet environments or with constrained computational resources, such as UAVs and small ground vehicles. Thus, deploying fine-tuned small language models (SLMs) that support onboard deployment offers a promising alternative. This paper introduces Ro-SLM, a framework that enables reliable SLM-driven robot operation by distilling LLMs' knowledge and reasoning. Ro-SLM starts from dataset synthesis by leveraging LLMs to generate diverse task instructions, produce corresponding ground truth code with minimal human assistance, and augment instructions into real-world application scenarios. Ro-SLM is then fine-tuned with the dataset, in which LLM serves as a reward function to guide the training. Extensive experiments on UAV operation tasks demonstrate that Ro-SLM improves the performance of SLM from being incapable of supporting robotic task planning and code generation to achieving performance that approaches LLM.

</details>


### [196] [Fast-SegSim: Real-Time Open-Vocabulary Segmentation for Robotics in Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10951)
*Xuan Yu,Yuxuan Xie,Shichao Zhai,Shuhao Ye,Rong Xiong,Yue Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Fast-SegSim enables real-time open-vocabulary 3D panoptic reconstruction using optimized 2D Gaussian Splatting with 40+ FPS rendering, addressing robotic control loop latency requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D reconstruction methods (NeRF, Gaussian Splatting variants) struggle with real-time inference frequencies needed for robotic control loops, especially when processing high-dimensional features for open-vocabulary segmentation.

Method: Built upon 2D Gaussian Splatting with two key optimizations: Precise Tile Intersection to reduce rasterization redundancy, and Top-K Hard Selection strategy leveraging geometric sparsity to simplify feature accumulation and alleviate bandwidth limitations.

Result: Achieves render rates exceeding 40 FPS, provides 3D-consistent outputs for multi-view ground truth labels, and when used to fine-tune perception modules in object goal navigation, successfully doubles the navigation success rate.

Conclusion: Fast-SegSim bridges the sim-to-real gap by providing real-time, high-fidelity open-vocabulary segmentation reconstruction with practical utility for robotic applications as both high-frequency sensor input and training data source.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary panoptic reconstruction is crucial for advanced robotics and simulation. However, existing 3D reconstruction methods, such as NeRF or Gaussian Splatting variants, often struggle to achieve the real-time inference frequency required by robotic control loops. Existing methods incur prohibitive latency when processing the high-dimensional features required for robust open-vocabulary segmentation. We propose Fast-SegSim, a novel, simple, and end-to-end framework built upon 2D Gaussian Splatting, designed to realize real-time, high-fidelity, and 3D-consistent open-vocabulary segmentation reconstruction. Our core contribution is a highly optimized rendering pipeline that specifically addresses the computational bottleneck of high-channel segmentation feature accumulation. We introduce two key optimizations: Precise Tile Intersection to reduce rasterization redundancy, and a novel Top-K Hard Selection strategy. This strategy leverages the geometric sparsity inherent in the 2D Gaussian representation to greatly simplify feature accumulation and alleviate bandwidth limitations, achieving render rates exceeding 40 FPS. Fast-SegSim provides critical value in robotic applications: it serves both as a high-frequency sensor input for simulation platforms like Gazebo, and its 3D-consistent outputs provide essential multi-view 'ground truth' labels for fine-tuning downstream perception tasks. We demonstrate this utility by using the generated labels to fine-tune the perception module in object goal navigation, successfully doubling the navigation success rate. Our superior rendering speed and practical utility underscore Fast-SegSim's potential to bridge the sim-to-real gap.

</details>


### [197] [Diffusion Reinforcement Learning Based Online 3D Bin Packing Spatial Strategy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10953)
*Jie Han,Tong Li,Qingyang Xu,Yong Song,Bao Pang,Xianfeng Yuan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A diffusion reinforcement learning algorithm for online 3D bin packing that outperforms state-of-the-art DRL methods by improving sample efficiency and packing density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online 3D bin packing is crucial for logistics, warehousing, and intelligent manufacturing. Current deep reinforcement learning approaches suffer from low sample efficiency, limiting their practical application in complex online scenarios.

Method: Proposes a diffusion reinforcement learning-based algorithm using: 1) Markov decision chain for packing modeling, 2) height map-based state representation, and 3) diffusion model-based actor network to improve policy learning.

Result: The algorithm significantly improves the average number of packed items compared to state-of-the-art DRL methods, demonstrating excellent application potential in complex online scenarios.

Conclusion: The diffusion reinforcement learning approach effectively addresses sample efficiency challenges in online 3D bin packing, offering superior performance and practical applicability for logistics and manufacturing applications.

Abstract: The online 3D bin packing problem is important in logistics, warehousing and intelligent manufacturing, with solutions shifting to deep reinforcement learning (DRL) which faces challenges like low sample efficiency. This paper proposes a diffusion reinforcement learning-based algorithm, using a Markov decision chain for packing modeling, height map-based state representation and a diffusion model-based actor network. Experiments show it significantly improves the average number of packed items compared to state-of-the-art DRL methods, with excellent application potential in complex online scenarios.

</details>


### [198] [ScoRe-Flow: Complete Distributional Control via Score-Based Reinforcement Learning for Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10962)
*Xiaotian Qiu,Lukai Chen,Jinhao Li,Qi Sun,Cheng Zhuo,Guohao Dai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ScoRe-Flow: A score-based RL fine-tuning method for flow matching policies that uses drift modulation via score functions and learned variance prediction to improve exploration efficiency and surpass suboptimal demonstration behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow Matching policies trained via imitation learning inherit limitations of demonstration data and cannot surpass suboptimal behaviors. While recent methods convert deterministic flows to SDEs with noise injection for exploration, this noise-only control compromises training efficiency when demonstrations already provide strong priors.

Method: Proposes ScoRe-Flow, a score-based RL fine-tuning method that modulates drift via the score function (gradient of log-density) to steer exploration toward high-probability regions. Combines drift modulation with learned variance prediction to achieve decoupled control over mean and variance of stochastic transitions. The score admits closed-form expression from velocity field, requiring no auxiliary networks.

Result: Achieves 2.4x faster convergence than flow-based SOTA on D4RL locomotion tasks and up to 5.4% higher success rates on Robomimic and Franka Kitchen manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Score-based drift modulation combined with learned variance prediction provides an effective approach for RL fine-tuning of Flow Matching policies, enabling efficient exploration and surpassing suboptimal demonstration behaviors while maintaining training efficiency.

Abstract: Flow Matching (FM) policies have emerged as an efficient backbone for robotic control, offering fast and expressive action generation that underpins recent large-scale embodied AI systems. However, FM policies trained via imitation learning inherit the limitations of demonstration data; surpassing suboptimal behaviors requires reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning. Recent methods convert deterministic flows into stochastic differential equations (SDEs) with learnable noise injection, enabling exploration and tractable likelihoods, but such noise-only control can compromise training efficiency when demonstrations already provide strong priors. We observe that modulating the drift via the score function, i.e., the gradient of log-density, steers exploration toward high-probability regions, improving stability. The score admits a closed-form expression from the velocity field, requiring no auxiliary networks. Based on this, we propose ScoRe-Flow, a score-based RL fine-tuning method that combines drift modulation with learned variance prediction to achieve decoupled control over the mean and variance of stochastic transitions. Experiments demonstrate that ScoRe-Flow achieves 2.4x faster convergence than flow-based SOTA on D4RL locomotion tasks and up to 5.4% higher success rates on Robomimic and Franka Kitchen manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [199] [Ψ-Map: Panoptic Surface Integrated Mapping Enables Real2Sim Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10982)
*Xuan Yu,Yuxuan Xie,Changjian Jiang,Shichao Zhai,Rong Xiong,Yu Zhang,Yue Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A real-time open-vocabulary panoptic reconstruction framework using LiDAR-enhanced Gaussian representations with end-to-end panoptic learning and optimized rendering for large-scale robotic scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D Gaussian Splatting methods fail to simultaneously achieve geometric accuracy, coherent panoptic understanding, and real-time inference in large-scale scenes, which is essential for advanced robotics perception and simulation.

Method: Three key components: 1) LiDAR-based plane-constrained multimodal GMMs with 2D Gaussian surfels for geometric reinforcement, 2) Query-guided end-to-end learning architecture with local cross-attention to lift 2D mask features to 3D, 3) Precise Tile Intersection and Top-K Hard Selection for rendering optimization.

Result: Superior geometric and panoptic reconstruction quality in large-scale scenes while maintaining inference rate exceeding 40 FPS, meeting real-time robotic control requirements.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully integrates geometric reinforcement, end-to-end panoptic learning, and efficient rendering to achieve real-time open-vocabulary panoptic reconstruction suitable for robotics applications.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary panoptic reconstruction is essential for advanced robotics perception and simulation. However, existing methods based on 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) often struggle to simultaneously achieve geometric accuracy, coherent panoptic understanding, and real-time inference frequency in large-scale scenes. In this paper, we propose a comprehensive framework that integrates geometric reinforcement, end-to-end panoptic learning, and efficient rendering. First, to ensure physical realism in large-scale environments, we leverage LiDAR data to construct plane-constrained multimodal Gaussian Mixture Models (GMMs) and employ 2D Gaussian surfels as the map representation, enabling high-precision surface alignment and continuous geometric supervision. Building upon this, to overcome the error accumulation and cumbersome cross-frame association inherent in traditional multi-stage panoptic segmentation pipelines, we design a query-guided end-to-end learning architecture. By utilizing a local cross-attention mechanism within the view frustum, the system lifts 2D mask features directly into 3D space, achieving globally consistent panoptic understanding. Finally, addressing the computational bottlenecks caused by high-dimensional semantic features, we introduce Precise Tile Intersection and a Top-K Hard Selection strategy to optimize the rendering pipeline. Experimental results demonstrate that our system achieves superior geometric and panoptic reconstruction quality in large-scale scenes while maintaining an inference rate exceeding 40 FPS, meeting the real-time requirements of robotic control loops.

</details>


### [200] [Inferring World Belief States in Dynamic Real-World Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11020)
*Jack Kolb,Aditya Garg,Nikolai Warner,Karen M. Feigh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Estimating human's world belief state from robot observations in dynamic 3D environments using mental model theory for human-robot team navigation and active assistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human decision making, contextual reasoning, situation awareness, and behavior planning rely on internal world belief states. In teams, mental models include team models of teammates' beliefs and capabilities, enabling fluent teamwork without constant explicit communication. The paper aims to replicate a core component of team models by inferring a teammate's belief state (level one situation awareness) for human-robot team navigation.

Method: Grounded in mental model theory, the approach infers human teammate's belief state from robot observations in dynamic, 3D, partially observable household environments. Methods are evaluated in realistic simulation, extended to real-world robot platform, and demonstrated through downstream active assistance semantic reasoning task.

Result: The paper presents methods for belief state estimation that work in realistic simulation environments and are successfully extended to real-world robot platforms. The downstream application demonstrates practical utility through active assistance semantic reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: The research successfully demonstrates estimation of human belief states in human-robot teams, enabling more fluent teamwork without constant explicit communication. The approach bridges simulation to real-world applications and shows practical value through active assistance capabilities.

Abstract: We investigate estimating a human's world belief state using a robot's observations in a dynamic, 3D, and partially observable environment. The methods are grounded in mental model theory, which posits that human decision making, contextual reasoning, situation awareness, and behavior planning draw from an internal simulation or world belief state. When in teams, the mental model also includes a team model of each teammate's beliefs and capabilities, enabling fluent teamwork without the need for constant and explicit communication. In this work we replicate a core component of the team model by inferring a teammate's belief state, or level one situation awareness, as a human-robot team navigates a household environment. We evaluate our methods in a realistic simulation, extend to a real-world robot platform, and demonstrate a downstream application of the belief state through an active assistance semantic reasoning task.

</details>


### [201] [Federated Single-Agent Robotics: Multi-Robot Coordination Without Intra-Robot Multi-Agent Fragmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11028)
*Xue Qin,Simin Luan,John See,Cong Yang,Zhijun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FSAR proposes a federated single-agent architecture for multi-robot coordination where each robot remains a single agent with local autonomy, and coordination emerges through fleet-level federation rather than intra-robot multi-agent decomposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As robot fleets scale, existing approaches fragment robots internally into multiple agents. The authors argue this is unnecessary and propose keeping each robot as a single embodied agent while achieving coordination through federation.

Method: Federated Single-Agent Robotics (FSAR) architecture with single-agent robot runtimes exposing governed capability surfaces. Coordination uses shared capability registries, cross-robot task delegation, policy-aware authority assignment, trust-scoped interaction, and layered recovery protocols.

Result: Statistically significant gains in governance locality (d=2.91, p<.001 vs. centralized control) and recovery containment (d=4.88, p<.001 vs. decomposition-heavy), with reduced authority conflicts and policy violations across all scenarios.

Conclusion: The path from embodied agents to embodied fleets is better served by federation across coherent robot runtimes than by fragmentation within them, supporting the FSAR approach to multi-robot coordination.

Abstract: As embodied robots move toward fleet-scale operation, multi-robot coordination is becoming a central systems challenge. Existing approaches often treat this as motivation for increasing internal multi-agent decomposition within each robot. We argue for a different principle: multi-robot coordination does not require intra-robot multi-agent fragmentation. Each robot should remain a single embodied agent with its own persistent runtime, local policy scope, capability state, and recovery authority, while coordination emerges through federation across robots at the fleet level. We present Federated Single-Agent Robotics (FSAR), a runtime architecture for multi-robot coordination built on single-agent robot runtimes. Each robot exposes a governed capability surface rather than an internally fragmented agent society. Fleet coordination is achieved through shared capability registries, cross-robot task delegation, policy-aware authority assignment, trust-scoped interaction, and layered recovery protocols. We formalize key coordination relations including authority delegation, inter-robot capability requests, local-versus-fleet recovery boundaries, and hierarchical human supervision, and describe a fleet runtime architecture supporting shared Embodied Capability Module (ECM) discovery, contract-aware cross-robot coordination, and fleet-level governance. We evaluate FSAR on representative multi-robot coordination scenarios against decomposition-heavy baselines. Results show statistically significant gains in governance locality (d=2.91, p<.001 vs. centralized control) and recovery containment (d=4.88, p<.001 vs. decomposition-heavy), while reducing authority conflicts and policy violations across all scenarios. Our results support the view that the path from embodied agents to embodied fleets is better served by federation across coherent robot runtimes than by fragmentation within them.

</details>


### [202] [Simulator Adaptation for Sim-to-Real Learning of Legged Locomotion via Proprioceptive Distribution Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11090)
*Jeremy Dao,Alan Fern*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proprioceptive distribution matching enables practical simulator adaptation for sim-to-real transfer of legged locomotion policies without motion capture or time alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation-trained legged locomotion policies suffer performance loss on hardware due to dynamics discrepancies between simulator and real world. Existing approaches require motion capture, privileged sensing, and careful initial conditions for time-aligned trajectory matching, making them impractical.

Method: Introduces proprioceptive distribution matching that compares hardware and simulation rollouts as distributions of joint observations and actions, eliminating need for time alignment or external sensing. Uses this metric as black-box objective to adapt simulator dynamics through parameter identification, action-delta models, and residual actuator models.

Result: Matches parameter recovery and policy-performance gains of privileged state-matching baselines in sim-to-sim ablations on Go2 quadruped. Real-world experiments show substantial drift reduction using less than five minutes of hardware data, even for challenging two-legged walking behavior.

Conclusion: Proprioceptive distribution matching provides a practical and effective route to simulator adaptation for sim-to-real transfer of learned legged locomotion, offering comparable performance to privileged methods without requiring motion capture or time alignment.

Abstract: Simulation trained legged locomotion policies often exhibit performance loss on hardware due to dynamics discrepancies between the simulator and the real world, highlighting the need for approaches that adapt the simulator itself to better match hardware behavior. Prior work typically quantify these discrepancies through precise, time-aligned matching of joint and base trajectories. This process requires motion capture, privileged sensing, and carefully controlled initial conditions. We introduce a practical alternative based on proprioceptive distribution matching, which compares hardware and simulation rollouts as distributions of joint observations and actions, eliminating the need for time alignment or external sensing. Using this metric as a black-box objective, we explore adapting simulator dynamics through parameter identification, action-delta models, and residual actuator models. Our approach matches the parameter recovery and policy-performance gains of privileged state-matching baselines across extensive sim-to-sim ablations on the Go2 quadruped. Real-world experiments demonstrate substantial drift reduction using less than five minutes of hardware data, even for a challenging two-legged walking behavior. These results demonstrate that proprioceptive distribution matching provides a practical and effective route to simulator adaptation for sim-to-real transfer of learned legged locomotion.

</details>


### [203] [AIM: Intent-Aware Unified world action Modeling with Spatial Value Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11135)
*Liaoyuan Fan,Zetian Xu,Chen Cao,Wenyao Zhang,Mingqi Yuan,Jiayu Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AIM is an intent-aware unified world action model that bridges video generation models and robot control by predicting spatial value maps that encode task-relevant interaction structure, enabling more reliable action generation without extensive robot-specific training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained video generation models provide strong priors for robot control, but existing unified world action models struggle to decode reliable actions without substantial robot-specific training due to structural mismatch - video models capture scene evolution while action generation requires explicit reasoning about interaction locations and manipulation intent.

Method: AIM introduces an explicit spatial interface that predicts aligned spatial value maps encoding task-relevant interaction structure instead of decoding actions directly from future visual representations. Built on a pretrained video generation model, it uses a shared mixture-of-transformers architecture with intent-causal attention to route future information to the action branch exclusively through value representation. Includes self-distillation reinforcement learning that freezes video/value branches and optimizes only the action head using dense rewards from projected value-map responses and sparse task-level signals.

Result: Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0 benchmark show AIM achieves 94.0% average success rate, significantly outperforming prior unified world action baselines. Improvements are more pronounced in long-horizon and contact-sensitive manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Explicit spatial-intent modeling serves as an effective bridge between visual world modeling and robot control, addressing the structural mismatch in unified world action models and enabling more reliable action generation from pretrained video priors.

Abstract: Pretrained video generation models provide strong priors for robot control, but existing unified world action models still struggle to decode reliable actions without substantial robot-specific training. We attribute this limitation to a structural mismatch: while video models capture how scenes evolve, action generation requires explicit reasoning about where to interact and the underlying manipulation intent. We introduce AIM, an intent-aware unified world action model that bridges this gap via an explicit spatial interface. Instead of decoding actions directly from future visual representations, AIM predicts an aligned spatial value map that encodes task-relevant interaction structure, enabling a control-oriented abstraction of future dynamics. Built on a pretrained video generation model, AIM jointly models future observations and value maps within a shared mixture-of-transformers architecture. It employs intent-causal attention to route future information to the action branch exclusively through the value representation. We further propose a self-distillation reinforcement learning stage that freezes the video and value branches and optimizes only the action head using dense rewards derived from projected value-map responses together with sparse task-level signals. To support training and evaluation, we construct a simulation dataset of 30K manipulation trajectories with synchronized multi-view observations, actions, and value-map annotations. Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0 benchmark show that AIM achieves a 94.0% average success rate, significantly outperforming prior unified world action baselines. Notably, the improvement is more pronounced in long-horizon and contact-sensitive manipulation tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of explicit spatial-intent modeling as a bridge between visual world modeling and robot control.

</details>


### [204] [ViserDex: Visual Sim-to-Real for Robust Dexterous In-hand Reorientation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11138)
*Arjun Bhardwaj,Maximum Wilder-Smith,Mayank Mittal,Vaishakh Patil,Marco Hutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A sim-to-real framework for monocular RGB in-hand object reorientation using 3D Gaussian Splatting for domain randomization and curriculum-based RL with teacher-student distillation, enabling robust manipulation on consumer hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-hand object reorientation requires precise pose estimation, but existing RGB-based solutions rely on multi-camera setups or costly ray tracing. There's a need for a practical monocular RGB approach that can handle complex task dynamics and bridge the visual sim-to-real gap efficiently.

Method: The framework integrates 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) for domain randomization in Gaussian representation space, applying physically consistent, pre-rendering augmentations to generate photorealistic randomized visual data. The manipulation policy is trained using curriculum-based reinforcement learning with teacher-student distillation. Both perception and control models can be trained independently on consumer-grade hardware.

Result: The pose estimator trained with 3DGS data outperforms those using conventional rendering data in challenging visual environments. The system was validated on a physical multi-fingered hand with RGB camera, demonstrating robust reorientation of five diverse objects under challenging lighting conditions.

Conclusion: Gaussian splatting provides a practical path for RGB-only dexterous manipulation, enabling efficient sim-to-real transfer with monocular sensing while eliminating the need for large compute clusters.

Abstract: In-hand object reorientation requires precise estimation of the object pose to handle complex task dynamics. While RGB sensing offers rich semantic cues for pose tracking, existing solutions rely on multi-camera setups or costly ray tracing. We present a sim-to-real framework for monocular RGB in-hand reorientation that integrates 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) to bridge the visual sim-to-real gap. Our key insight is performing domain randomization in the Gaussian representation space: by applying physically consistent, pre-rendering augmentations to 3D Gaussians, we generate photorealistic, randomized visual data for object pose estimation. The manipulation policy is trained using curriculum-based reinforcement learning with teacher-student distillation, enabling efficient learning of complex behaviors. Importantly, both perception and control models can be trained independently on consumer-grade hardware, eliminating the need for large compute clusters. Experiments show that the pose estimator trained with 3DGS data outperforms those trained using conventional rendering data in challenging visual environments. We validate the system on a physical multi-fingered hand equipped with an RGB camera, demonstrating robust reorientation of five diverse objects even under challenging lighting conditions. Our results highlight Gaussian splatting as a practical path for RGB-only dexterous manipulation. For videos of the hardware deployments and additional supplementary materials, please refer to the project website: https://rffr.leggedrobotics.com/works/viserdex/

</details>


### [205] [EmbodiedGovBench: A Benchmark for Governance, Recovery, and Upgrade Safety in Embodied Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11174)
*Xue Qin,Simin Luan,John See,Cong Yang,Zhijun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EmbodiedGovBench is a benchmark for evaluating governance aspects of embodied AI systems beyond task success, focusing on controllability, policy enforcement, safety, and auditability under realistic perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current embodied AI evaluation focuses primarily on task success metrics (completion rate, manipulation accuracy), leaving a critical gap in measuring whether systems are governable - whether they respect capability boundaries, enforce policies, recover safely, maintain audit trails, and respond to human oversight.

Method: The authors present EmbodiedGovBench, a benchmark structure covering seven governance dimensions: unauthorized capability invocation, runtime drift robustness, recovery success, policy portability, version upgrade safety, human override responsiveness, and audit completeness. The benchmark includes scenario templates, perturbation operators, governance metrics, and baseline evaluation protocols, designed to be instantiated over embodied capability runtimes with modular interfaces and contract-aware upgrade workflows.

Result: The benchmark provides a comprehensive measurement framework for governance-oriented evaluation of embodied agent systems, enabling assessment of whether systems remain controllable, policy-bounded, recoverable, auditable, and evolution-safe under realistic perturbations in both single-robot and fleet settings.

Conclusion: Embodied governance should become a first-class evaluation target in embodied AI research, and EmbodiedGovBench provides the initial measurement framework to facilitate this shift from purely task-oriented evaluation to comprehensive governance assessment.

Abstract: Recent progress in embodied AI has produced a growing ecosystem of robot policies, foundation models, and modular runtimes. However, current evaluation remains dominated by task success metrics such as completion rate or manipulation accuracy. These metrics leave a critical gap: they do not measure whether embodied systems are governable -- whether they respect capability boundaries, enforce policies, recover safely, maintain audit trails, and respond to human oversight. We present EmbodiedGovBench, a benchmark for governance-oriented evaluation of embodied agent systems. Rather than asking only whether a robot can complete a task, EmbodiedGovBench evaluates whether the system remains controllable, policy-bounded, recoverable, auditable, and evolution-safe under realistic perturbations. The benchmark covers seven governance dimensions: unauthorized capability invocation, runtime drift robustness, recovery success, policy portability, version upgrade safety, human override responsiveness, and audit completeness. We define a benchmark structure spanning single-robot and fleet settings, with scenario templates, perturbation operators, governance metrics, and baseline evaluation protocols. We describe how the benchmark can be instantiated over embodied capability runtimes with modular interfaces and contract-aware upgrade workflows. Our analysis suggests that embodied governance should become a first-class evaluation target. EmbodiedGovBench provides the initial measurement framework for that shift.

</details>


### [206] [CLAW: Composable Language-Annotated Whole-body Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11251)
*Jianuo Cao,Yuxin Chen,Masayoshi Tomizuka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CLAW is an interactive web-based pipeline for scalable generation of language-annotated whole-body motion data for humanoid robots, addressing limitations of existing motion capture and text-to-motion approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for training language-conditioned whole-body controllers face two main limitations: motion capture is costly and limited in diversity, while text-to-motion generative models produce purely kinematic outputs that are not guaranteed to be physically feasible.

Method: CLAW treats motion modes of a kinematic planner as composable building blocks parameterized by movement, heading, speed, pelvis height and duration. It provides two browser-based interfaces (real-time keyboard mode and timeline-based sequence editor) for data collection. A low-level whole-body controller tracks kinematic references in MuJoCo simulation to produce physically grounded trajectories, while a deterministic template-based annotation engine generates diverse natural-language descriptions at multiple stylistic registers.

Result: The system enables scalable generation of language-motion paired data for the Unitree G1 humanoid robot, producing physically grounded trajectories recorded at 50Hz with diverse natural-language annotations.

Conclusion: CLAW provides an open-source solution for scalable generation of language-annotated whole-body motion data, addressing key limitations in existing approaches and supporting humanoid robot learning research.

Abstract: Training language-conditioned whole-body controllers for humanoid robots requires large-scale datasets pairing motion trajectories with natural-language descriptions.Existing approaches based on motion capture are costly and limited in diversity, while text-to-motion generative models produce purely kinematic outputs that are not guaranteed to be physically feasible.Therefore, we present CLAW, an interactive web-based pipeline for scalable generation of language-annotated whole-body motion data for the Unitree G1 humanoid robot. CLAW treats the motion modes of a kinematic planner as composable building blocks, each parameterized by movement, heading, speed, pelvis height and duration, and provides two browser-based interfaces -- a real-time keyboard mode and a timeline-based sequence editor -- for exploratory and batch data collection. A low-level whole-body controller tracks the planner's kinematic references in MuJoCo simulation, producing physically grounded trajectories recorded at 50Hz. Simultaneously, a deterministic template-based annotation engine generates diverse natural-language descriptions at multiple stylistic registers for every segment and for the full trajectory. We release the system as open source to support scalable generation of language-motion paired data for humanoid robot learning.

</details>


### [207] [Modeling, Analysis and Activation of Planar Viscoelastically-combined Rimless Wheels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11295)
*Fumihiko Asano,Yuxuan Xiang,Yanqiu Zheng,Cong Yan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes novel passive-dynamic walkers called viscoelastically-combined rimless wheels (VCRWs) formed by two cross-shaped frames connected by eight viscoelastic elements, with two variants (VCRW1 and VCRW2) analyzed for gait properties and potential as walking support devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop novel passive-dynamic walkers that combine two four-legged rimless wheels via viscoelastic elements, creating a new type of walking mechanism that could serve as a walking support device.

Method: Proposes VCRW models with two variants: VCRW1 (Greek-cross-shaped frames) and VCRW2 (two-link cross-shaped frames with free rotation). Describes model assumptions, equations of motion and collision, numerically analyzes passive dynamic walking gait properties, and considers activation of VCRW2 for stable level gait generation.

Result: Numerical analysis of basic gait properties for passive dynamic walking, with consideration of VCRW2 activation for generating stable level gait, demonstrating the potential of these novel walkers.

Conclusion: The VCRW represents a novel walking mechanism with potential applications as a walking support device, combining rimless wheel concepts with viscoelastic elements to create stable passive-dynamic walkers.

Abstract: This paper proposes novel passive-dynamic walkers formed by two cross-shaped frames and eight viscoelastic elements. Since it is a combination of two four-legged rimless wheels via viscoelastic elements, we call it viscoelastically-combined rimless wheel (VCRW). Two types of VCRWs consisting of different cross-shaped frames are introduced; one is formed by combining two Greek-cross-shaped frames (VCRW1), and the other is formed by combining two-link cross-shaped frames that can rotate freely around the central axis (VCRW2). First, we describe the model assumptions and equations of motion and collision. Second, we numerically analyze the basic gait properties of passive dynamic walking. Furthermore, we consider an activation of VCRW2 for generating a stable level gait, and discuss the significance of the study as a novel walking support device.

</details>


### [208] [3D-Anchored Lookahead Planning for Persistent Robotic Scene Memory via World-Model-Based MCTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11302)
*Bronislav Sidik,Dror Mizrahi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 3D-ALP is a robotic manipulation planning system that combines MCTS with a 3D-consistent world model, enabling spatial memory and lookahead planning for tasks requiring memory of occluded objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reactive policies fail at sequential manipulation tasks requiring spatial memory when objects become occluded, as they only evaluate actions from the current camera frame without maintaining persistent world state.

Method: Combines Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with a 3D-consistent world model as rollout oracle, maintains persistent camera-to-world anchor that survives occlusion, and resolves four structural failure modes in applying UCT-MCTS to continuous robotic manipulation.

Result: On 5-step sequential reach task requiring spatial memory: 3D-ALP achieves 0.650±0.109 success on memory-required steps vs 0.006±0.008 for greedy baseline (Δ=+0.645). Step 5 success reaches 0.822 vs 0.000 for greedy. Tree search spatial memory accounts for +0.533 (82% of gain) with deeper lookahead adding +0.111 (17%).

Conclusion: 3D-ALP demonstrates that combining MCTS with persistent 3D world models enables effective spatial memory and lookahead planning for robotic manipulation, significantly outperforming reactive baselines on tasks requiring memory of occluded objects.

Abstract: We present 3D-Anchored Lookahead Planning (3D-ALP), a System 2 reasoning engine for robotic manipulation that combines Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with a 3D-consistent world model as the rollout oracle. Unlike reactive policies that evaluate actions from the current camera frame only, 3D-ALP maintains a persistent camera-to-world (c2w) anchor that survives occlusion, enabling accurate replanning to object positions that are no longer directly observable. On a 5-step sequential reach task requiring spatial memory (Experiment E3), 3D-ALP achieves 0.650 0.109 success rate on memory-required steps versus 0.006 0.008 for a greedy reactive baseline (Δ=+0.645), while step 5 success reaches 0.822 against 0.000 for greedy. An ablation study (30 episodes, 3 seeds) isolates tree search spatial memory as the primary driver (+0.533, 82% of gain) with additional benefit from deeper lookahead (+0.111, 17%). We also identify and resolve four structural failure modes in applying UCT-MCTS (Upper Confidence Bounds applied to Trees [10]) to continuous robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [209] [Learning to Forget -- Hierarchical Episodic Memory for Lifelong Robot Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11306)
*Leonard Bärmann,Joana Plewnia,Alex Waibel,Tamim Asfour*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: H²-EMV enables humanoid robots to learn selective forgetting through user interaction, constructing hierarchical episodic memory that adapts to user-specific relevance, reducing memory size by 45% while improving question-answering accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots need to verbalize past experiences but maintaining lifelong episodic memory from continuous multimodal perception exceeds storage limits and makes real-time query impractical, requiring selective forgetting that adapts to users' notions of relevance.

Method: Incrementally constructs hierarchical episodic memory, selectively forgets using language-model-based relevance estimation conditioned on learned natural-language rules, and updates these rules given user feedback about forgotten details.

Result: Reduces memory size by 45% and query-time compute by 35% while maintaining question-answering accuracy. Performance improves over time with 70% accuracy increase in second-round queries by adapting to user-specific priorities.
