Conclusion: Learned forgetting enables scalable, personalized episodic memory for long-term human-robot collaboration, demonstrating that robots can learn what to remember through user interaction and adapt to user-specific relevance criteria.

Abstract: Robots must verbalize their past experiences when users ask "Where did you put my keys?" or "Why did the task fail?" Yet maintaining life-long episodic memory (EM) from continuous multimodal perception quickly exceeds storage limits and makes real-time query impractical, calling for selective forgetting that adapts to users' notions of relevance. We present H$^2$-EMV, a framework enabling humanoids to learn what to remember through user interaction. Our approach incrementally constructs hierarchical EM, selectively forgets using language-model-based relevance estimation conditioned on learned natural-language rules, and updates these rules given user feedback about forgotten details. Evaluations on simulated household tasks and 20.5-hour-long real-world recordings from ARMAR-7 demonstrate that H$^2$-EMV maintains question-answering accuracy while reducing memory size by 45% and query-time compute by 35%. Critically, performance improves over time - accuracy increases 70% in second-round queries by adapting to user-specific priorities - demonstrating that learned forgetting enables scalable, personalized EM for long-term human-robot collaboration.

</details>


### [210] [CLASP: Closed-loop Asynchronous Spatial Perception for Open-vocabulary Desktop Object Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11320)
*Yiran Ling,Wenxuan Li,Siying Dong,Yize Zhang,Xiaoyao Huang,Jing Jiang,Ruonan Li,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CLASP is a closed-loop framework for robot grasping that integrates multimodal perception with state-reflective feedback to address spatial hallucination and open-loop fragility in VLM-based manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models show promise for robotic manipulation but face three key challenges in low-level grasping: scarce high-quality multimodal demonstrations, spatial hallucination due to weak geometric grounding, and fragility of open-loop execution in dynamic environments.

Method: Three main components: 1) Dual-Pathway Hierarchical Perception module that decouples semantic intent from geometric grounding to reduce spatial illusions; 2) Asynchronous Closed-Loop Evaluator that compares pre-/post-execution states with text-based diagnostic feedback for error correction; 3) Scalable multimodal data engine that automatically synthesizes spatial annotations and reasoning templates without human teleoperation.

Result: Achieves 87.0% overall success rate, significantly outperforming existing baselines. Demonstrates remarkable generalization across diverse objects, bridges sim-to-real gap, and shows exceptional robustness in geometrically challenging categories and cluttered scenarios.

Conclusion: CLASP effectively addresses key limitations of VLM-based grasping through its closed-loop architecture, hierarchical perception design, and automated data synthesis, providing a robust solution for real-world robotic manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Robot grasping of desktop object is widely used in intelligent manufacturing, logistics, and agriculture.Although vision-language models (VLMs) show strong potential for robotic manipulation, their deployment in low-level grasping faces key challenges: scarce high-quality multimodal demonstrations, spatial hallucination caused by weak geometric grounding, and the fragility of open-loop execution in dynamic environments. To address these challenges, we propose Closed-Loop Asynchronous Spatial Perception(CLASP), a novel asynchronous closed-loop framework that integrates multimodal perception, logical reasoning, and state-reflective feedback. First, we design a Dual-Pathway Hierarchical Perception module that decouples high-level semantic intent from geometric grounding. The design guides the output of the inference model and the definite action tuples, reducing spatial illusions. Second, an Asynchronous Closed-Loop Evaluator is implemented to compare pre- and post-execution states, providing text-based diagnostic feedback to establish a robust error-correction loop and improving the vulnerability of traditional open-loop execution in dynamic environments. Finally, we design a scalable multi-modal data engine that automatically synthesizes high-quality spatial annotations and reasoning templates from real and synthetic scenes without human teleoperation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving an 87.0% overall success rate. Notably, the proposed framework exhibits remarkable generalization across diverse objects, bridging the sim-to-real gap and providing exceptional robustness in geometrically challenging categories and cluttered scenarios.

</details>


### [211] [Learning Racket-Ball Bounce Dynamics Across Diverse Rubbers for Robotic Table Tennis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11349)
*Thomas Gossard*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified framework for modeling ball-racket interactions across different rubber types using Gaussian Processes conditioned on impact state, improving prediction accuracy and enabling online identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing racket-ball bounce models are limited to inverted rubbers and use simple linear assumptions, failing to generalize across the diverse racket configurations encountered in practice, particularly different rubber types like anti-spin and pimpled surfaces.

Method: Collected dataset of racket-ball bounces across 10 racket configurations using high-speed multi-camera setup with spin estimation. Estimated parameters of an impulse-based contact model using Gaussian Processes conditioned on the ball's incoming velocity and spin, providing both predictions and uncertainty estimations.

Result: Reduced post-impact velocity and spin prediction errors across all racket types compared to constant parameter baselines, with largest improvements for nonstandard rubbers. The GP-based model enables online identification of racket dynamics with few observations during gameplay.

Conclusion: The proposed unified framework successfully models ball-racket interactions across diverse rubber types, capturing systematic variations in physical parameters with impact state while maintaining physical interpretability and enabling practical applications in robotic table tennis.

Abstract: Accurate dynamic models for racket-ball bounces are essential for reliable control in robotic table tennis. Existing models typically assume simple linear models and are restricted to inverted rubbers, limiting their ability to generalize across the wide variety of rackets encountered in practice. In this work, we present a unified framework for modeling ball-racket interactions across 10 racket configurations featuring different rubber types, including inverted, anti-spin, and pimpled surfaces. Using a high-speed multi-camera setup with spin estimation, we collect a dataset of racket-ball bounces spanning a broad range of incident velocities and spins. We show that key physical parameters governing rebound, such as the Coefficient of Restitution and tangential impulse response, vary systematically with the impact state and differ significantly across rubbers. To capture these effects while preserving physical interpretability, we estimate the parameters of an impulse-based contact model using Gaussian Processes conditioned on the ball's incoming velocity and spin. The resulting model provides both accurate predictions and uncertainty estimations. Compared to the constant parameter baselines, our approach reduces post-impact velocity and spin prediction errors across all racket types, with the largest improvements observed for nonstandard rubbers. Furthermore, the GP-based model enables online identification of racket dynamics with few observations during gameplay.

</details>


### [212] [WM-DAgger: Enabling Efficient Data Aggregation for Imitation Learning with World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11351)
*Anlan Yu,Zaishu Chen,Peili Song,Zhiqing Hong,Haotian Wang,Desheng Zhang,Tian He,Yi Ding,Daqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WM-DAgger is an imitation learning framework that uses World Models to synthesize out-of-distribution recovery data without human involvement, addressing compounding errors in robotic manipulation tasks with few-shot demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning suffers from compounding errors where minor policy inaccuracies lead to out-of-distribution states and eventual failures. While DAgger addresses this, it requires continuous human involvement which limits scalability.

Method: WM-DAgger leverages World Models to synthesize OOD recovery data. It introduces two key mechanisms: (1) Corrective Action Synthesis Module for task-oriented recovery actions to prevent misleading supervision, and (2) Consistency-Guided Filtering Module that discards physically implausible trajectories by anchoring terminal synthesized frames to real expert demonstration frames.

Result: The method significantly improves success rates, achieving 93.3% success rate in soft bag pushing with only five demonstrations. Extensive validation on multiple real-world robotic tasks demonstrates effectiveness.

Conclusion: WM-DAgger provides an efficient data aggregation framework that overcomes the scalability limitations of traditional DAgger by using World Models to synthesize recovery data without human involvement, while addressing hallucination issues through corrective action synthesis and consistency-guided filtering.

Abstract: Imitation learning is a powerful paradigm for training robotic policies, yet its performance is limited by compounding errors: minor policy inaccuracies could drive robots into unseen out-of-distribution (OOD) states in the training set, where the policy could generate even bigger errors, leading to eventual failures. While the Data Aggregation (DAgger) framework tries to address this issue, its reliance on continuous human involvement severely limits scalability. In this paper, we propose WM-DAgger, an efficient data aggregation framework that leverages World Models to synthesize OOD recovery data without requiring human involvement. Specifically, we focus on manipulation tasks with an eye-in-hand robotic arm and only few-shot demonstrations. To avoid synthesizing misleading data and overcome the hallucination issues inherent to World Models, our framework introduces two key mechanisms: (1) a Corrective Action Synthesis Module that generates task-oriented recovery actions to prevent misleading supervision, and (2) a Consistency-Guided Filtering Module that discards physically implausible trajectories by anchoring terminal synthesized frames to corresponding real frames in expert demonstrations. We extensively validate WM-DAgger on multiple real-world robotic tasks. Results that our method significantly improves success rates, achieving a 93.3\% success rate in soft bag pushing with only five demonstrations. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/czs12354-xxdbd/WM-Dagger.

</details>


### [213] [MR.ScaleMaster: Scale-Consistent Collaborative Mapping from Crowd-Sourced Monocular Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11372)
*Hyoseok Ju,Giseop Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MR.ScaleMaster is a cooperative mapping system for crowd-sourced monocular videos that addresses scale collapse and drift issues through a Scale Collapse Alarm, Sim(3) anchor nodes for per-session scale estimation, and a plug-and-play interface for integration with existing monocular SLAM systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crowd-sourced cooperative mapping from monocular cameras faces two critical scale-specific failure modes: abrupt scale collapse from false-positive loop closures in repetitive environments, and gradual scale drift over long trajectories with per-robot scale ambiguity that prevents direct multi-session fusion.

Method: Three key mechanisms: 1) Scale Collapse Alarm to reject spurious loop closures before corrupting the pose graph; 2) Sim(3) anchor node formulation generalizing classical SE(3) framework to explicitly estimate per-session scale, resolving per-robot scale ambiguity and enforcing global scale consistency; 3) Modular, open-source, plug-and-play interface enabling any monocular reconstruction model to integrate without backend modification.

Result: On KITTI sequences with up to 15 agents, the Sim(3) formulation achieves 7.2x ATE reduction over SE(3) baseline, and the alarm rejects all false-positive loops while preserving every valid constraint. Demonstrated heterogeneous multi-robot dense mapping fusing MASt3R-SLAM, pi3, and VGGT-SLAM 2.0 within a single unified map.

Conclusion: MR.ScaleMaster effectively addresses both scale collapse and drift in crowd-sourced cooperative monocular mapping, enabling robust multi-agent, multi-session reconstruction through its scale-aware formulation and false-positive loop closure rejection mechanism.

Abstract: Crowd-sourced cooperative mapping from monocular cameras promises scalable 3D reconstruction without specialized sensors, yet remains hindered by two scale-specific failure modes: abrupt scale collapse from false-positive loop closures in repetitive environments, and gradual scale drift over long trajectories and per-robot scale ambiguity that prevent direct multi-session fusion. We present MR.ScaleMaster, a cooperative mapping system for crowd-sourced monocular videos that addresses both failure modes. MR.ScaleMaster introduces three key mechanisms. First, a Scale Collapse Alarm rejects spurious loop closures before they corrupt the pose graph. Second, a Sim(3) anchor node formulation generalizes the classical SE(3) framework to explicitly estimate per-session scale, resolving per-robot scale ambiguity and enforcing global scale consistency. Third, a modular, open-source, plug-and-play interface enables any monocular reconstruction model to integrate without backend modification. On KITTI sequences with up to 15 agents, the Sim(3) formulation achieves a 7.2x ATE reduction over the SE(3) baseline, and the alarm rejects all false-positive loops while preserving every valid constraint. We further demonstrate heterogeneous multi-robot dense mapping fusing MASt3R-SLAM, pi3, and VGGT-SLAM 2.0 within a single unified map.

</details>


### [214] [Minimal Embodiment Enables Efficient Learning of Number Concepts in Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11373)
*Zhegong Shangguan,Alessandro Di Nuovo,Angelo Cangelosi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Embodied neural network achieves 96.8% counting accuracy with only 10% training data vs 60.6% for vision-only, develops biologically plausible numerical representations through robotic interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots need to understand abstract numerical concepts for human-interactive scenarios, but how intelligent systems acquire numerical concepts from sensorimotor experience remains a fundamental challenge in cognitive science and AI.

Method: Neural network model trained to perform sequential counting through naturalistic robotic interaction with a Franka Panda manipulator, comparing embodied vs vision-only baselines.

Result: Embodied models achieve 96.8% counting accuracy with only 10% of training data vs 60.6% for vision-only; develops number-selective units with logarithmic tuning, mental number line organization, Weber-law scaling, and rotational dynamics (r = 0.97, slope = 30.6°/count).

Conclusion: Minimal embodiment grounds abstract concepts, improves data efficiency, yields interpretable representations aligned with biological cognition, with applications to embodied mathematics tutoring and safety-critical industrial applications.

Abstract: Robots are increasingly entering human-interactive scenarios that require understanding of quantity. How intelligent systems acquire abstract numerical concepts from sensorimotor experience remains a fundamental challenge in cognitive science and artificial intelligence. Here we investigate embodied numerical learning using a neural network model trained to perform sequential counting through naturalistic robotic interaction with a Franka Panda manipulator. We demonstrate that embodied models achieve 96.8\% counting accuracy with only 10\% of training data, compared to 60.6\% for vision-only baselines. This advantage persists when visual-motor correspondences are randomized, indicating that embodiment functions as a structural prior that regularizes learning rather than as an information source. The model spontaneously develops biologically plausible representations: number-selective units with logarithmic tuning, mental number line organization, Weber-law scaling, and rotational dynamics encoding numerical magnitude ($r = 0.97$, slope $= 30.6°$/count). The learning trajectory parallels children's developmental progression from subset-knowers to cardinal-principle knowers. These findings demonstrate that minimal embodiment can ground abstract concepts, improve data efficiency, and yield interpretable representations aligned with biological cognition, which may contribute to embodied mathematics tutoring and safety-critical industrial applications.

</details>


### [215] [ComSim: Building Scalable Real-World Robot Data Generation via Compositional Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11386)
*Yiran Qin,Jiahua Ma,Li Kang,Wenzhan Li,Yihang Jiao,Xin Wen,Xiufeng Song,Heng Zhou,Jiwen Yu,Zhenfei Yin,Xihui Liu,Philip Torr,Yilun Du,Ruimao Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Compositional Simulation combines classical and neural simulation to generate accurate action-video pairs, using a real-sim-real pipeline to create diverse training data that reduces the sim2real gap in robotics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale, high-quality training data for robotics is difficult to acquire due to manual effort requirements and limited coverage of diverse real-world environments, creating a bottleneck for foundational model applications in robotics.

Method: A hybrid Compositional Simulation approach using a closed-loop real-sim-real data augmentation pipeline that leverages small real-world datasets to generate diverse, large-scale training data, with neural simulation transforming classical simulation videos into real-world representations.

Result: The method significantly reduces the sim2real domain gap, resulting in higher success rates for real-world policy model training compared to conventional approaches.

Conclusion: Compositional Simulation offers a scalable solution for generating robust training data and bridging the gap between simulated and real-world robotics, enabling more effective use of foundational models in robotics applications.

Abstract: Recent advancements in foundational models, such as large language models and world models, have greatly enhanced the capabilities of robotics, enabling robots to autonomously perform complex tasks. However, acquiring large-scale, high-quality training data for robotics remains a challenge, as it often requires substantial manual effort and is limited in its coverage of diverse real-world environments. To address this, we propose a novel hybrid approach called Compositional Simulation, which combines classical simulation and neural simulation to generate accurate action-video pairs while maintaining real-world consistency. Our approach utilizes a closed-loop real-sim-real data augmentation pipeline, leveraging a small amount of real-world data to generate diverse, large-scale training datasets that cover a broader spectrum of real-world scenarios. We train a neural simulator to transform classical simulation videos into real-world representations, improving the accuracy of policy models trained in real-world environments. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that our method significantly reduces the sim2real domain gap, resulting in higher success rates in real-world policy model training. Our approach offers a scalable solution for generating robust training data and bridging the gap between simulated and real-world robotics.

</details>


### [216] [EagleVision: A Multi-Task Benchmark for Cross-Domain Perception in High-Speed Autonomous Racing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11400)
*Zakhar Yagudin,Murad Mebrahtu,Ren Jin,Jiaqi Huang,Yujia Yue,Dzmitry Tsetserukou,Jorge Dias,Majid Khonji*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EagleVision is a unified LiDAR-based benchmark for 3D detection and trajectory prediction in high-speed racing, featuring annotated data from Indy Autonomous Challenge, A2RL Real competition, and simulator, with cross-domain generalization analysis showing urban pretraining benefits detection and Indy-trained models excel at trajectory prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-speed autonomous racing presents extreme perception challenges with large relative velocities and domain shifts not captured by existing benchmarks, necessitating a specialized evaluation framework for these high-dynamic conditions.

Method: Introduces EagleVision benchmark with newly annotated 3D bounding boxes for Indy Autonomous Challenge (14,893 frames), A2RL Real competition (1,163 frames), and 12,000 simulator-generated frames, standardized under common evaluation protocol. Uses dataset-centric transfer framework to quantify cross-domain generalization across urban, simulator, and real racing domains.

Result: Urban pretraining improves detection over scratch training (NDS 0.72 vs. 0.69), while intermediate pretraining on real racing data achieves best transfer to A2RL (NDS 0.726). For trajectory prediction, Indy-trained models surpass in-domain A2RL training on A2RL test sequences (FDE 0.947 vs. 1.250), highlighting motion-distribution coverage importance.

Conclusion: EagleVision enables systematic study of perception generalization under extreme high-speed dynamics, showing that domain transfer strategies and motion-distribution coverage are crucial for high-speed racing perception. The benchmark is publicly available for community use.

Abstract: High-speed autonomous racing presents extreme perception challenges, including large relative velocities and substantial domain shifts from conventional urban-driving datasets. Existing benchmarks do not adequately capture these high-dynamic conditions. We introduce EagleVision, a unified LiDAR-based multi-task benchmark for 3D detection and trajectory prediction in high-speed racing, providing newly annotated 3D bounding boxes for the Indy Autonomous Challenge dataset (14,893 frames) and the A2RL Real competition dataset (1,163 frames), together with 12,000 simulator-generated annotated frames, all standardized under a common evaluation protocol. Using a dataset-centric transfer framework, we quantify cross-domain generalization across urban, simulator, and real racing domains. Urban pretraining improves detection over scratch training (NDS 0.72 vs. 0.69), while intermediate pretraining on real racing data achieves the best transfer to A2RL (NDS 0.726), outperforming simulator-only adaptation. For trajectory prediction, Indy-trained models surpass in-domain A2RL training on A2RL test sequences (FDE 0.947 vs. 1.250), highlighting the role of motion-distribution coverage in cross-domain forecasting. EagleVision enables systematic study of perception generalization under extreme high-speed dynamics. The dataset and benchmark are publicly available at https://avlab.io/EagleVision

</details>


### [217] [Using Unwrapped Full Color Space Palette Recording to Measure Exposedness of a Vehicle Exterior Parts for External Human Machine Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11406)
*Jaerock Kwon,Jose Gonzalez-Belmonte*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper analyzes optimal placement locations for External Human-Machine Interfaces (eHMIs) on autonomous vehicles by simulating pedestrian visibility at intersections using 3D modeling and Unity engine simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous vehicles need to communicate intent to pedestrians to prevent accidents, but current eHMI placement strategies may be insufficient due to visibility obstructions from other vehicles at intersections.

Method: Used mesh unwrapping technique to create unique color-coded textures for a 2015 Ford F-150 King Ranch model, then ran animated simulations in Unity game engine to measure visibility frequency of each vehicle point to crossing pedestrians at four-way intersections.

Result: While bumper, grill, and hood were most frequently visible to crossing pedestrians, these locations become insufficient when other vehicles in the same lane obstruct the view. The study found these traditional placements inadequate for reliable communication.

Conclusion: Recommends a distributive approach to eHMI placement using both windshield and frontal fenders simultaneously to ensure visibility even when traditional front-facing locations are obstructed by other vehicles.

Abstract: One of the concerns with autonomous vehicles is their ability to communicate their intent to other road users, specially pedestrians, in order to prevent accidents. External Human-Machine Interfaces (eHMIs) are the proposed solution to this issue, through the introduction of electronic devices on the exterior of a vehicle that communicate when the vehicle is planning on slowing down or yielding. This paper uses the technique of unwrapping the faces of a mesh onto a texture where every pixel is a unique color, as well as a series of animated simulations made and ran in the Unity game engine, to measure how many times is each point on a 2015 Ford F-150 King Ranch is unobstructed to a pedestrian attempting to cross the road at a four-way intersection. By cross-referencing the results with a color-coded map of the labeled parts on the exterior of the vehicle, it was concluded that while the bumper, grill, and hood were the parts of the vehicle visible to the crossing pedestrian most often, the existence of other vehicles on the same lane that might obstruct the view of these makes them insufficient. The study recommends instead a distributive approach to eHMIs by using both the windshield and frontal fenders as simultaneous placements for these devices.

</details>


### [218] [Efficient Emotion-Aware Iconic Gesture Prediction for Robot Co-Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11417)
*Edwin C. Montiel-Vazquez,Christian Arzate Cruz,Stefanos Gkikas,Thomas Kassiotis,Giorgos Giannakakis,Randy Gomez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Lightweight transformer generates iconic gestures from text and emotion without audio, outperforming GPT-4o on semantic placement and intensity while being computationally efficient for real-time robot deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current data-driven robot gesture systems primarily produce rhythmic beat-like motions but lack semantic emphasis, which is important since co-speech gestures enhance engagement and speech understanding.

Method: Proposes a lightweight transformer model that derives iconic gesture placement and intensity from text and emotion inputs alone, requiring no audio input during inference.

Result: The model outperforms GPT-4o in both semantic gesture placement classification and intensity regression on the BEAT2 dataset, while remaining computationally compact and suitable for real-time deployment on embodied agents.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully addresses the limitation of semantic emphasis in robot gesture generation by creating an efficient, audio-free system that generates meaningful iconic gestures from text and emotion.

Abstract: Co-speech gestures increase engagement and improve speech understanding. Most data-driven robot systems generate rhythmic beat-like motion, yet few integrate semantic emphasis. To address this, we propose a lightweight transformer that derives iconic gesture placement and intensity from text and emotion alone, requiring no audio input at inference time. The model outperforms GPT-4o in both semantic gesture placement classification and intensity regression on the BEAT2 dataset, while remaining computationally compact and suitable for real-time deployment on embodied agents.

</details>


### [219] [Dyadic Partnership(DP): A Missing Link Towards Full Autonomy in Medical Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11423)
*Nassir Navab,Zhongliang Jiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces Dyadic Partnership (DP) as a new paradigm for medical robotics, moving beyond tele-manipulation toward intelligent collaboration between robots and clinicians using generative AI and advanced visualization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical robots are limited to tele-manipulation, leaving cognition and decision-making to surgeons. As robotics advances toward autonomy, there's a need for a pathway that enables intelligent collaboration between humans and robots in clinical settings.

Method: Proposes Dyadic Partnership paradigm involving intelligent expert interaction where robots and clinicians collaboratively discuss and agree on decisions using generative AI (world models), multi-modal intent recognition, co-learning frameworks, advanced visualization, and explainable trust-aware interaction.

Result: Introduces foundational components for DP systems including clinical intelligence foundation models, multi-modal intent recognition, co-learning frameworks, advanced visualization, and explainable interaction mechanisms.

Conclusion: Dyadic Partnership represents an achievable, acceptable milestone offering a promising pathway toward safer, more intuitive collaboration and gradual transition to full autonomy in diverse clinical settings, though challenges like data scarcity, standardization, and ethical acceptance remain.

Abstract: For the past decades medical robotic solutions were mostly based on the concept of tele-manipulation. While their design was extremely intelligent, allowing for better access, improved dexterity, reduced tremor, and improved imaging, their intelligence was limited. They therefore left cognition and decision making to the surgeon. As medical robotics advances towards high-level autonomy, the scientific community needs to explore the required pathway towards partial and full autonomy. Here, we introduce the concept of Dyadic Partnership(DP), a new paradigm in which robots and clinicians engage in intelligent, expert interaction and collaboration. The Dyadic Partners would discuss and agree on decisions and actions during their dynamic and interactive collaboration relying also on intuitive advanced media using generative AI, such as a world model, and advanced multi-modal visualization. This article outlines the foundational components needed to enable such systems, including foundation models for clinical intelligence, multi-modal intent recognition, co-learning frameworks, advanced visualization, and explainable, trust-aware interaction. We further discuss key challenges such as data scarcity, lack of standardization, and ethical acceptance. Dyadic partnership is introduced and is positioned as a powerful yet achievable, acceptable milestone offering a promising pathway toward safer, more intuitive collaboration and a gradual transition to full autonomy across diverse clinical settings.

</details>


### [220] [Safe Human-to-Humanoid Motion Imitation Using Control Barrier Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11447)
*Wenqi Cai,John Abanes,Nikolaos Evangeliou,Anthony Tzes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Vision-based framework for humanoid robots to imitate human movements while preventing collisions using Control Barrier Functions


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensuring operational safety is critical for human-to-humanoid motion imitation, particularly preventing collisions during the imitation process

Method: Human skeletal keypoints captured by single camera → converted to joint angles for motion retargeting → safety enforced via Control Barrier Function (CBF) layer formulated as Quadratic Program (QP) that filters imitation commands to prevent self-collisions and human-robot collisions

Result: Simulation results validate effectiveness of proposed framework for real-time collision-aware motion imitation

Conclusion: The vision-based framework with CBF-based safety layer enables safe human-to-humanoid motion imitation by preventing collisions while maintaining real-time performance

Abstract: Ensuring operational safety is critical for human-to-humanoid motion imitation. This paper presents a vision-based framework that enables a humanoid robot to imitate human movements while avoiding collisions. Human skeletal keypoints are captured by a single camera and converted into joint angles for motion retargeting. Safety is enforced through a Control Barrier Function (CBF) layer formulated as a Quadratic Program (QP), which filters imitation commands to prevent both self-collisions and human-robot collisions. Simulation results validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework for real-time collision-aware motion imitation.

</details>


### [221] [DA-PTQ: Drift-Aware Post-Training Quantization for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11572)
*Siyuan Xu,Tianshi Wang,Fengling Li,Lei Zhu,Heng Tao Shen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DA-PTQ addresses quantization-induced performance degradation in Vision-Language-Action models by mitigating temporal error accumulation through drift-aware optimization, enabling efficient deployment on resource-limited robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLAs face deployment challenges on resource-limited robots due to high memory/computational demands. Direct PTQ application causes severe performance degradation in sequential control tasks due to temporal error accumulation and kinematic drift.

Method: DA-PTQ formulates quantization as drift-aware optimization with two components: Cross-Space Representation Compensation to mitigate structured distortions between multimodal representations and action space, and Motion-Driven Mixed-Precision Allocation that assigns bit-widths by minimizing trajectory-level motion errors.

Result: Extensive experiments show DA-PTQ significantly reduces kinematic drift and achieves comparable performance to full-precision models under low-bit settings, enabling practical VLA deployment on resource-limited robotic platforms.

Conclusion: DA-PTQ effectively addresses the temporal error accumulation problem in VLA quantization, providing a practical solution for deploying complex multimodal models on resource-constrained robotic systems while maintaining control performance.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) have demonstrated strong potential for embodied AI, yet their deployment on resource-limited robots remains challenging due to high memory and computational demands. While Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) provides an efficient solution, directly applying PTQ to VLAs often results in severe performance degradation during sequential control. We identify temporal error accumulation as a key factor, where quantization perturbations at the vision-language-to-action interface are progressively amplified, leading to kinematic drift in executed trajectories. To address this issue, we propose Drift-Aware Post-Training Quantization (DA-PTQ), which formulates quantization as a drift-aware optimization problem over sequential decision processes. DA-PTQ consists of two components: (1) Cross-Space Representation Compensation, which mitigates structured distortions between multimodal representations and action space to improve action consistency, and (2) Motion-Driven Mixed-Precision Allocation, which assigns bit-widths by minimizing trajectory-level motion errors. Extensive experiments show that DA-PTQ significantly reduces kinematic drift and achieves comparable performance to full-precision models under low-bit settings, enabling practical deployment of VLAs on resource-limited robotic platforms.

</details>


### [222] [Micro-Dexterity in Biological Micromanipulation: Embodiment, Perception, and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11640)
*Kangyi Lu,Lan Wei,Zongcai Tan,Dandan Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This review paper introduces "micro-dexterity" as a framework for analyzing biological micromanipulation, examining how classical manipulation primitives are reformulated at microscale, comparing different manipulation platforms, and identifying challenges for clinical translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical applications require precise and adaptive interaction with biological micro-objects, but microscale manipulation faces unique challenges due to fluidic environments, negligible inertia, dominant interfacial forces, and fragile targets that make classical dexterous manipulation assumptions difficult to maintain.

Method: The review introduces micro-dexterity as an analytical framework based on embodiment, perception, and control. It examines how classical manipulation primitives (pushing, reorientation, grasping, cooperative manipulation) are reformulated at microscale, compares three main platform architectures (embodied microrobots, field-mediated systems, externally actuated end-effectors), and reviews required perception and control strategies.

Result: The analysis reveals that microscale manipulation operates in fundamentally different regimes than macroscale manipulation, requiring new approaches to handle fluidic environments, surface-dominated physics, and soft/fragile biological targets. Different platform architectures offer trade-offs between contact-based and contactless manipulation.

Conclusion: There is a significant dexterity gap between current laboratory demonstrations and clinically relevant biological manipulation. Future translation requires addressing key challenges in adapting classical manipulation concepts to microscale physics and biological constraints.

Abstract: Microscale manipulation has advanced substantially in controlled locomotion and targeted transport, yet many biomedical applications require precise and adaptive interaction with biological micro-objects. At these scales, manipulation is realized through three main classes of platforms: embodied microrobots that physically interact as mobile agents, field-mediated systems that generate contactless trapping or manipulation forces, and externally actuated end-effectors that interact through remotely driven physical tools. Unlike macroscale manipulators, these systems function in fluidic, confined, and surface-dominated environments characterized by negligible inertia, dominant interfacial forces, and soft, heterogeneous, and fragile targets. Consequently, classical assumptions of dexterous manipulation, including rigid-body contact, stable grasping, and rich proprioceptive feedback, become difficult to maintain. This review introduces micro-dexterity as a framework for analyzing biological micromanipulation through the coupled roles of embodiment, perception, and control. We examine how classical manipulation primitives, including pushing, reorientation, grasping, and cooperative manipulation, are reformulated at the microscale; compare the architectures that enable them, from contact-based micromanipulators to contactless field-mediated systems and cooperative multi-agent platforms; and review the perception and control strategies required for task execution. We identify the current dexterity gap between laboratory demonstrations and clinically relevant biological manipulation, and outline key challenges for future translation.

</details>


### [223] [AffordSim: A Scalable Data Generator and Benchmark for Affordance-Aware Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11674)
*Mingyang Li,Haofan Xu,Haowen Sun,Xinzhe Chen,Sihua Ren,Liqi Huang,Xinyang Sui,Chenyang Miao,Qiongjie Cui,Zeyang Liu,Xingyu Chen,Xuguang Lan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AffordSim integrates open-vocabulary 3D affordance prediction into robotic manipulation data generation, enabling automated generation of semantically correct trajectories for tasks requiring precise interaction with functional object regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing simulation platforms lack object affordance information in trajectory generation, preventing automatic generation of semantically correct trajectories for tasks requiring precise interaction with specific functional regions (e.g., grasping a mug by its handle, pouring from a cup's rim).

Method: AffordSim framework integrates VoxAfford (open-vocabulary 3D affordance detector) with MLLM output tokens enhanced by multi-scale geometric features to predict affordance maps on object point clouds, guiding grasp pose estimation. Built on NVIDIA Isaac Sim with cross-embodiment support, VLM-powered task generation, and novel domain randomization using DA3-based 3D Gaussian reconstruction from real photographs.

Result: Benchmark of 50 tasks across 7 categories shows grasping largely solved (53-93% success), but affordance-demanding tasks like pouring into narrow containers (1-43%) and mug hanging (0-47%) remain challenging for current imitation learning methods. Zero-shot sim-to-real experiments on real Franka FR3 validate transferability.

Conclusion: AffordSim enables automated, scalable generation of affordance-aware manipulation data, highlighting the need for affordance-aware data generation as current imitation learning methods struggle with tasks requiring precise functional region interaction.

Abstract: Simulation-based data generation has become a dominant paradigm for training robotic manipulation policies, yet existing platforms do not incorporate object affordance information into trajectory generation. As a result, tasks requiring precise interaction with specific functional regions--grasping a mug by its handle, pouring from a cup's rim, or hanging a mug on a hook--cannot be automatically generated with semantically correct trajectories. We introduce AffordSim, the first simulation framework that integrates open-vocabulary 3D affordance prediction into the manipulation data generation pipeline. AffordSim uses our VoxAfford model, an open-vocabulary 3D affordance detector that enhances MLLM output tokens with multi-scale geometric features, to predict affordance maps on object point clouds, guiding grasp pose estimation toward task-relevant functional regions. Built on NVIDIA Isaac Sim with cross-embodiment support (Franka FR3, Panda, UR5e, Kinova), VLM-powered task generation, and novel domain randomization using DA3-based 3D Gaussian reconstruction from real photographs, AffordSim enables automated, scalable generation of affordance-aware manipulation data. We establish a benchmark of 50 tasks across 7 categories (grasping, placing, stacking, pushing/pulling, pouring, mug hanging, long-horizon composite) and evaluate 4 imitation learning baselines (BC, Diffusion Policy, ACT, Pi 0.5). Our results reveal that while grasping is largely solved (53-93% success), affordance-demanding tasks such as pouring into narrow containers (1-43%) and mug hanging (0-47%) remain significantly more challenging for current imitation learning methods, highlighting the need for affordance-aware data generation. Zero-shot sim-to-real experiments on a real Franka FR3 validate the transferability of the generated data.

</details>


### [224] [Dual-Control Frequency-Aware Diffusion Model for Depth-Dependent Optical Microrobot Microscopy Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11680)
*Lan Wei,Zongcai Tan,Kangyi Lu,Jian-Qing Zheng,Dandan Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Du-FreqNet: A dual-control, frequency-aware diffusion model for physically consistent microscopy image synthesis of optical microrobots, addressing data scarcity and improving depth-dependent optical characteristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous operation of optical microrobots requires accurate 3D perception, but development is hindered by scarce high-quality microscopy datasets due to complex fabrication and labor-intensive annotation. Existing GAN-based generative methods fail to reproduce key optical characteristics like depth-dependent diffraction and defocus effects.

Method: Proposes Du-FreqNet, a dual-control, frequency-aware diffusion model with two independent ControlNet branches: one for encoding microrobot 3D point clouds and another for depth-specific mesh layers. Introduces adaptive frequency-domain loss that dynamically reweights high- and low-frequency components based on distance to focal plane, using differentiable FFT-based supervision to capture physically meaningful frequency distributions.

Result: Trained on limited datasets (e.g., 80 images per pose), achieves controllable, depth-dependent image synthesis with 20.7% SSIM improvement over baselines. Generalizes effectively to unseen poses and significantly enhances downstream tasks including 3D pose and depth estimation.

Conclusion: Du-FreqNet enables physically consistent microscopy image synthesis for optical microrobots, facilitating robust closed-loop control in microrobotic systems by overcoming data scarcity and improving reproduction of optical characteristics.

Abstract: Optical microrobots actuated by optical tweezers (OT) are important for cell manipulation and microscale assembly, but their autonomous operation depends on accurate 3D perception. Developing such perception systems is challenging because large-scale, high-quality microscopy datasets are scarce, owing to complex fabrication processes and labor-intensive annotation. Although generative AI offers a promising route for data augmentation, existing generative adversarial network (GAN)-based methods struggle to reproduce key optical characteristics, particularly depth-dependent diffraction and defocus effects. To address this limitation, we propose Du-FreqNet, a dual-control, frequency-aware diffusion model for physically consistent microscopy image synthesis. The framework features two independent ControlNet branches to encode microrobot 3D point clouds and depth-specific mesh layers, respectively. We introduce an adaptive frequency-domain loss that dynamically reweights high- and low-frequency components based on the distance to the focal plane. By leveraging differentiable FFT-based supervision, Du-FreqNet captures physically meaningful frequency distributions often missed by pixel-space methods. Trained on a limited dataset (e.g., 80 images per pose), our model achieves controllable, depth-dependent image synthesis, improving SSIM by 20.7% over baselines. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Du-FreqNet generalizes effectively to unseen poses and significantly enhances downstream tasks, including 3D pose and depth estimation, thereby facilitating robust closed-loop control in microrobotic systems.

</details>


### [225] [ACT: Automated CPS Testing for Open-Source Robotic Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11708)
*Aditya A. Krishnan,Donghoon Kim,Hokeun Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ACT framework automates continuous testing of open-source CPS software with robotic platforms via GitHub integration


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source CPS software lacks robust testing with robotic platforms, leading to undetected critical errors, especially when multiple modules are developed by various contributors

Method: Proposed Automated CPS Testing (ACT) framework that performs automated, continuous testing of open-source software with robotic platforms, integrated with open-source infrastructure like GitHub

Result: Implemented an ACT prototype and conducted a case study on an open-source CPS with an educational robotic platform to demonstrate its capabilities

Conclusion: ACT addresses the testing gap in open-source CPS development by providing automated, continuous testing integrated with existing open-source infrastructure

Abstract: Open-source software for cyber-physical systems (CPS) often lacks robust testing involving robotic platforms, resulting in critical errors that remain undetected. This is especially challenging when multiple modules of CPS software are developed by various open-source contributors. To address this gap, we propose Automated CPS Testing (ACT) that performs automated, continuous testing of open-source software with its robotic platforms, integrated with the open-source infrastructure such as GitHub. We implement an ACT prototype and conduct a case study on an open-source CPS with an educational robotic platform to demonstrate its capabilities.

</details>


### [226] [Grounded World Model for Semantically Generalizable Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11751)
*Quanyi Li,Lan Feng,Haonan Zhang,Wuyang Li,Letian Wang,Alexandre Alahi,Harold Soh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GWM-MPC learns a grounded world model in vision-language-aligned latent space, enabling Model Predictive Control to score action proposals based on similarity to task instructions rather than requiring goal images, achieving superior semantic generalization on novel tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional visuomotor MPC requires goal images in advance, which is challenging for new environments and offers limited interactivity compared to natural language instructions. Vision-language models (VLMs) for VLAs have poor semantic generalization despite overfitting training data.

Method: Learn a Grounded World Model (GWM) in a vision-language-aligned latent space where world models predict future outcomes and score action proposals based on embedding similarity to task instructions, transforming visuomotor MPC to a VLA.

Result: GWM-MPC achieves 87% success rate on WISER benchmark test set (288 tasks with unseen visual signals and referring expressions), while traditional VLAs average only 22% success despite 90% training set performance.

Conclusion: Learning world models in vision-language-aligned space enables MPC to effectively use natural language instructions without requiring goal images, achieving superior semantic generalization compared to VLM-based VLAs on novel tasks.

Abstract: In Model Predictive Control (MPC), world models predict the future outcomes of various action proposals, which are then scored to guide the selection of the optimal action. For visuomotor MPC, the score function is a distance metric between a predicted image and a goal image, measured in the latent space of a pretrained vision encoder like DINO and JEPA. However, it is challenging to obtain the goal image in advance of the task execution, particularly in new environments. Additionally, conveying the goal through an image offers limited interactivity compared with natural language. In this work, we propose to learn a Grounded World Model (GWM) in a vision-language-aligned latent space. As a result, each proposed action is scored based on how close its future outcome is to the task instruction, reflected by the similarity of embeddings. This approach transforms the visuomotor MPC to a VLA that surpasses VLM-based VLAs in semantic generalization. On the proposed WISER benchmark, GWM-MPC achieves a 87% success rate on the test set comprising 288 tasks that feature unseen visual signals and referring expressions, yet remain solvable with motions demonstrated during training. In contrast, traditional VLAs achieve an average success rate of 22%, even though they overfit the training set with a 90% success rate.

</details>


### [227] [StarVLA-$α$: Reducing Complexity in Vision-Language-Action Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11757)
*Jinhui Ye,Ning Gao,Senqiao Yang,Jinliang Zheng,Zixuan Wang,Yuxin Chen,Pengguang Chen,Yilun Chen,Shu Liu,Jiaya Jia*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: StarVLA-α is a simple yet strong Vision-Language-Action baseline model designed to study VLA design choices systematically, showing that minimal architectural complexity with a strong VLM backbone is sufficient for competitive performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Vision-Language-Action (VLA) landscape is fragmented with substantial variations in architectures, training data, embodiment configurations, and benchmark-specific engineering, making it difficult to systematically study design choices and understand what truly matters for performance.

Method: StarVLA-α minimizes architectural and pipeline complexity to reduce experimental confounders, enabling systematic analysis of key design axes including action modeling strategies, robot-specific pretraining, and interface engineering. The approach uses unified multi-benchmark training on LIBERO, SimplerEnv, RoboTwin, and RoboCasa.

Result: The simple baseline remains highly competitive across benchmarks, indicating that a strong VLM backbone combined with minimal design is sufficient for strong performance without additional architectural complexity. Notably, the single generalist model outperforms π₀.₅ by 20% on the public real-world RoboChallenge benchmark.

Conclusion: StarVLA-α serves as a solid starting point for future VLA research, demonstrating that simplicity and systematic design evaluation can yield strong performance without relying on complex engineering tricks or excessive architectural modifications.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for building general-purpose robotic agents. However, the VLA landscape remains highly fragmented and complex: as existing approaches vary substantially in architectures, training data, embodiment configurations, and benchmark-specific engineering. In this work, we introduce StarVLA-$α$, a simple yet strong baseline designed to study VLA design choices under controlled conditions. StarVLA-$α$ deliberately minimizes architectural and pipeline complexity to reduce experimental confounders and enable systematic analysis. Specifically, we re-evaluate several key design axes, including action modeling strategies, robot-specific pretraining, and interface engineering. Across unified multi-benchmark training on LIBERO, SimplerEnv, RoboTwin, and RoboCasa, the same simple baseline remains highly competitive, indicating that a strong VLM backbone combined with minimal design is already sufficient to achieve strong performance without relying on additional architectural complexity or engineering tricks. Notably, our single generalist model outperforms $π_{0.5}$ by 20\% on the public real-world RoboChallenge benchmark. We expect StarVLA-$α$ to serve as a solid starting point for future research in the VLA regime. Code will be released at https://github.com/starVLA/starVLA.

</details>


### [228] [Identifying Inductive Biases for Robot Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11768)
*Apoorv Vaish,Oliver Brock*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient co-design algorithm for robot morphology and control that identifies and leverages task-specific inductive biases from search data, achieving 36% more improvement and 100x sample efficiency over benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Co-designing robot morphology and control is a high-dimensional search problem that requires systematic methods to identify inductive biases that make the search tractable, similar to synergistic interactions found in biological organisms.

Method: Analyzed co-design landscapes for soft locomotion and manipulation tasks to identify consistent patterns across co-design space regions. Developed an algorithm that infers task-specific structure from search data and adapts to each task's specific patterns.

Result: The algorithm yields 36% more improvement than benchmark algorithms and achieves more than two orders of magnitude (100x) sample efficiency compared to benchmark algorithms.

Conclusion: Leveraging inductive biases tailored to co-design structure enables efficient robot morphology and control co-design, demonstrating the effectiveness of identifying and adapting to task-specific patterns discovered during search.

Abstract: Co-designing a robot's morphology and control can ensure synergistic interactions between them, prevalent in biological organisms. However, co-design is a high-dimensional search problem. To make this search tractable, we need a systematic method for identifying inductive biases tailored to its structure. In this paper, we analyze co-design landscapes for soft locomotion and manipulation tasks and identify three patterns that are consistent across regions of their co-design spaces. We observe that within regions of co-design space, quality varies along a low-dimensional manifold. Higher-quality regions exhibit variations spread across more dimensions, while tightly coupling morphology and control. We leverage these insights to devise an efficient co-design algorithm. Since the precise instantiation of this structure varies across tasks and is not known a priori, our algorithm infers it from information gathered during search and adapts to each task's specific structure. This yields $36\%$ more improvement than benchmark algorithms. Moreover, our algorithm achieved more than two orders of magnitude in sample efficiency compared to these benchmark algorithms, demonstrating the effectiveness of leveraging inductive biases to co-design.

</details>


### [229] [Disentangled Point Diffusion for Precise Object Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11793)
*Lyuxing He,Eric Cai,Shobhit Aggarwal,Jianjun Wang,David Held*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAX-DPD is a hierarchical point diffusion framework for robotic placement tasks that achieves state-of-the-art precision and generalization by combining global scene-level placement priors with local object-level geometric reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end policies for robotic manipulation struggle with generalization to novel object geometries and achieving high precision. Object-centric approaches that predict placement poses offer modular decomposition but need improvement in precision and geometric reasoning.

Method: Hierarchical disentangled point diffusion framework with two components: 1) Feed-forward Dense Gaussian Mixture Model for global scene-level placement priors, and 2) Disentangled point cloud diffusion that separately diffuses object geometry and placement frame for precise local geometric reasoning.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance in placement precision, multi-modal coverage, and generalization to variations in object geometries and scene configurations. Outperforms SE(3)-diffusion approaches for rigid object placement and shows potential for non-rigid tasks like cloth hanging.

Conclusion: TAX-DPD provides an effective hierarchical approach to robotic placement tasks that combines global scene understanding with precise local geometric reasoning, enabling high-precision manipulation with strong generalization capabilities across rigid and potentially non-rigid objects.

Abstract: Recent advances in robotic manipulation have highlighted the effectiveness of learning from demonstration. However, while end-to-end policies excel in expressivity and flexibility, they struggle both in generalizing to novel object geometries and in attaining a high degree of precision. An alternative, object-centric approach frames the task as predicting the placement pose of the target object, providing a modular decomposition of the problem. Building on this goal-prediction paradigm, we propose TAX-DPD, a hierarchical, disentangled point diffusion framework that achieves state-of-the-art performance in placement precision, multi-modal coverage, and generalization to variations in object geometries and scene configurations. We model global scene-level placements through a novel feed-forward Dense Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) that yields a spatially dense prior over global placements; we then model the local object-level configuration through a novel disentangled point cloud diffusion module that separately diffuses the object geometry and the placement frame, enabling precise local geometric reasoning. Interestingly, we demonstrate that our point cloud diffusion achieves substantially higher accuracy than a prior approach based on SE(3)-diffusion, even in the context of rigid object placement. We validate our approach across a suite of challenging tasks in simulation and in the real-world on high-precision industrial insertion tasks. Furthermore, we present results on a cloth-hanging task in simulation, indicating that our framework can further relax assumptions on object rigidity.

</details>
